INTRODUCTION Seven years ago I wrote a book calledThe Most Important Thing: Uncommon Sense for the Thoughtful Investor, regarding whereinvestors should direct their greatest attention. In it I said “the most impo being attentive to cycles.” The truth, however, is that I applied most important thing” to nineteen other thingsas well. Thereis no most important thing in investing. Everyone of the twenty elements discussed in The Most Important Thingis absolutely essential for anyo wishes to be a successful investor. VinceLombardi, the legendary coachof the GreenBay Packers, is fam for having said, “winning isn’t everything, it’s the only thing.” I’ve been able to figureout what Lombardi actually meantby that statement, there’s no doubt he considered winning the most important thing. Likew can’t say an understanding of cycles is everything in investing, thing, but for me it’s certainly right near the top of the list. Most of the great investors I’ve known over the years have had an exceptional sense for how cycles work in general and wherewe stand current one. That sense permits them to do a superior job of portfolios for what lies ahead. Good cycle timing—combined with an effective investment approach and the involvement of exceptional peop —hasaccounted for the vast bulk of the success of my firm, Oakt Management. It’s for that reason—and because I find something particularly int about the fluctuations of cycles—and because wherewe stand in the one of the thingsmy clients ask about most—and finally because been written about the essential nature of cycles—that I decid Most Important Thingwith a book devoted entirely to an exploration cycles. I hope you’llfind it of use. ∾ Some patterns and events recur regularly in our environment, influ behavior and our lives. The winter is colder and snowier than and the daytime is lighter than the night. Thus we plan ski trips

and sailing trips for the summer, and our work and recreation for and our sleeping at night. We turn on the lights as evening draw turn them off when we go to bed. We unpack our warm coats as approaches and our bathing suits for the summer. Whilesome people the oceanin winter for exhilarationand some elect to work the night free up their days, the vast majority of us follow the normal patterns, making everyday life easier. We humans use our ability to recognize and understand patterns our decisions easier, increase benefits and avoid pain. Importantly, on our knowledge of recurring patterns so we won’thave to recon decision from scratch. We know hurricanes are more likely in September, we avoid the Caribbean at that time of year. We New Yorkers sched visits to Miami and Phoenix for the winter months, when the differential is a positive, not a negative. And we don’t have to in January and decide anew whether to dress for warmth or Economies, companies and markets also operate pursuant to patterns. of these patterns are commonly calledcycles. They arise from naturally occurring phenomena but, importantly,also from the ups and downs of human psychology and from the resultant human behavior. Because psychology and behavior play such a big part in creating them, these aren’tas regular as the cycles of clock and calendar, but they better and worsetimes for certain actions. And they can profoundly affec investors. If we pay attention to cycles, we can come out ahead past cycles, understand their origins and import, and keep alert for one, we don’t have to reinvent the wheelin order to understand every investment environment anew.And we have less of a chance of blindsided by events. We can master these recurring patterns for betterment. ∾ It’s my primary message that we should pay attention to cycle should say “listen to them.” Dictionary.com supplies two close distinct definitions for the word “listen.” The first is “to attendclose purpose of hearing.” The second is “to heed.” Both definitions what I’m writing about. In order to properly position a portfolio for what’s going on environment—and for what that implies regarding the futureof the the investor has to maintain a high level of attention. Events to everyone who is operating in a given environment. But not to them equally in the sense of paying attention, being awareof thus potentially figuring out their import.

And certainly not everyone heeds equally. By “heed” I mean “o mind, be guided by or take to heart.” Or, in other words, and follow its dictates.” Perhaps I can better convey this “heed listening by listing its antonyms: ignore, disregard, discount, rejec neglect, shun, flout, disobey, tune out, turn a deaf ear to, or be Invariably, investors who disregard wherethey stand in cycles are boun sufferserious consequences. In order to get the most out of this book—and do the best job with cycles—an investor has to learn to recognize cycles, assessthem for the instructions they imply, and do what they tell him to do. author’s note belowregarding my use of male pronouns.) If an inves in this sense,he will be able to convert cycles from a wild, force that wreaks havoc, into a phenomenon that can be unde advantage of: a vein that can be mined for significant outperform ∾ A winning investment philosophy can be created only through the combination of a number of essential elements: A technical education in accounting, finance and economics provid foundation: necessary but far from sufficient. A view on how markets work is important—you should have you set out to invest, but it must be addedto, questioned, reshaped as you proceed. Some of your initialviewswill come from what you’ve read, so readin is an essential building block. Continuing to read will enable increase the efficacy of your approach—both embracing those ideas find appealing and discarding those you don’t.Importantly, it’s great read outside the strict boundaries of investing. Legendary investor Charlie Munger often pointsto the benefits of reading broadl and processes in other fields can add greatly to effective invest approaches and decisions. Exchangingideas with fellow investors can be an invaluable source growth. Giventhe non-scientific nature of investing, there’s thing as being finished with your learning, and no individual has monopoly on insight. Investing can be solitary, but I think practice it in solitude are missing a lot, both intellectually interpersonally. Finally, there really is no substitute for experience. Everyyear I come to view investing differently, and every cycle I’ve lived through has taught me something about how to cope with the next one. recommend a long careerand see no reason to stop any time

I WHY STUDYCYCLES? The odds change as our position in the cycles changes. I change our investment stance as these thingschange, we’re being passive regarding cycles; in other words, we’re ignoring the c tilt the odds in our favor.But if we apply some insight regardi cycles, we can increase our bets and place them on more aggress investmentswhen the odds are in our favor,and we can take money off the table and increase our defensiveness when the odds are a us. Investing is a matter of preparing for the financial future. define the task: we assemble portfolios today that we hope will bene the events that unfold in the years ahead. For professionalinvestors, success consists of doing this better than the average investor, or outperforming an assigned market benchmark performance of whichis determined by the actions of all the But achieving that kind of success is no small challenge: although easy to generate average investment performance, it’s quite hard to aboveaverage. One of the most important foundational elements of my investme philosophy is my conviction that we can’t know what the “macro futur in store for us in terms of thingslike economies, markets or geop put it more precisely, few people are able on balance to know macro futurethan others. And it’s only if we know more than other that consists of having better data; doing a superior job of interp we have; knowing what actions to take on the basis of or our having the emotional fortitude required to take those actions) that forecasts will lead to outperformance. In short, if we have the same information as others, analyze it way, reach the same conclusions and implement them the same way, we shouldn’t expect that process to result in outperformance. And it’s difficult to be consistentlysuperior in those regards as relates

So, in my view, tryingto predict what the macro futureholds is help investors achieve superior investment performance. Very few inves are known for having outperformed through macro forecasting Warren Buffett once told me about his two criteria for a de information: it has to be important, and it has to be know “everyone knows” that macro developments play a dominant role determiningthe performance of markets these days, “macro investors” wholehave shown ratherunimpressive results. It’s not that the macr matter, but ratherthat very few people can master it. For most knowable (or not knowable well enough and consistentlyenough for to outperformance). Thus I dismiss macro prediction as something that will bring inv success for the vast majority of investors, and I certainly inclu that group. If that’s so, what’s left? Whilethere are lots of detai nuances, I think we can most gainfully spendour time in three gene tryingto know more than othersabout what I call “the knowable”: the fundamentals of industries, companies and securities, being disciplined as to the appropriate price to pay for a partici those fundamentals, and understanding the investment environment we’re in and deciding strategicallyposition our portfolios for it. A great deal has been written on the first two topics. Together constitute the key ingredients in “security analysis” and “value inves judgments regarding what an asset can produce in the future—usually terms of earnings or cash flow—and what those prospects make the asset worthtoday. What do value investors do? They strive to take advantage of discrepa between “price” and “value.” In order to do that successfully, quantify an asset’s intrinsic value and how it’s likely to change (b) assesshow the current market price compares with the asset’s value,past pricesfor the asset, the pricesof other assets, and “theoretica fair” pricesfor assetsin general. Then they use that information to assemble portfolios. Most of the tim their immediate goal to hold investmentsoffering the best available value propositions: the assetswith the greatest upside potential and/or of upside potential to downside risk. You mightargue that assembling portfolio should consist of nothing more than identifying the asset highest value and the ones whose pricesmost understate their value.That be true in general and in the long term, but I think another

profitably enter into the process: properly positioning a portfolio for likely to happen in the market in the years immediately ahead In my view, the greatest way to optimize the positioning of a given point in time is through deciding what balance it should aggressiveness and defensiveness. And I believe the aggressiveness/defensiveness balance should be adjusted over time in response to changes in the state of the investment environment number of elements stand in their cycles. The key word is “calibrate.” The amount you have invested, your allocation of capital among the various possibilities, and th of the thingsyou own all should be calibrated along a continu runs from aggressive to defensive. . . . Whenwe’re getting v cheap, we should be aggressive; when we’re getting value expensi we should pull back. (“Yet Again?,” September 2017) Calibrating one’s portfolio position is what this book is mostly abou ∾ One of the key wordsrequired if one is to understand the reaso cycles is “tendencies.” If the factors that influence investing were regular and predictab example, if macro forecasting worked—we would be able to “will happen.” Yet the fact that that’s not the case doesn’t mean we’re helpless in contemplating the future. Rather, we can talk abou mighthappen or should happen, and how likely they are to happ thingsare what I call “tendencies.” In the investment world, we talk about risk all the time, but the universal agreement about what risk is or what it should implyfor behavior. Some people think risk is the likelihood of losingmoney, (including many finance academics) think risk is the volatility of asset or returns. And there are many other kinds of risk—too many to cove I lean heavily toward the first definition: in my view, risk is likelihood of permanent capital loss. But there’s also such a thing opportunity risk: the likelihood of missing out on potential gains.Put together and we see that risk is the possibility of thingsnot going want. What is the originof risk? One of my favorite investment philosop late Peter Bernstein, said in an issue of his Economics and Portfolio newsletter titled “Can We Measure Risk with a Number?” (June 2007):

Essentially risk says we don’t know what’s going to happen. . . walk every moment into the unknown. There’s a range of outcom and we don’t know where[the actualoutcome is] going to fall within the range. Often we don’t know what the range is. You’ll find belowa few ideas (summarized very briefly from the treatment provided in my memo “Risk Revisited Again” of June think follow directly from the starting point provided by Bernstein. mighthelp you understand and cope with risk. As retired London Business School professor Elroy Dimson sai means more thingscan happen than will happen.” For each event in economics, business and markets (among other things), if only one could happen—if there could be only one outcome—and if it was predi there would, of course, be no uncertainty or risk. And with no regarding what was going to happen, in theory we could know exact position our portfolios to avoid loss and garner maximum gains.But and in investing, since there can be many different outcomes, uncertainty risk are inescapable. As a consequence of the above, the futureshould be vie fixed outcome that’s destined to happen and capable of being predi a range of possibilitiesand—hopefully on the basis of insight into respective likelihoods—as a probability distribution.Probability distributions reflect one’s view of tendencies. Investors—or anyone hoping to deal successfullywith the future— form probability distributions, either explicitly or informally. If it’s done those probabilities will be helpful in determiningone’s proper cours action. But it’s still essential to bear in mind that even if we probabilities, that doesn’t mean we know what’s going to happen. Outcomes regarding a given matter may be governed by a pro distribution in the long run, but with regard to the outcome of there can be great uncertainty. Any of the outcomes included in a can occur, albeit with varying probabilities, since the process throu the outcome is chosen will be affected not only by the merit randomness. To invertDimson’s statement, even though many thingscan happen, only one will. We may know what to expect “on average,” may have no connection with what actually will happen. In my way of thinking about it, investment success is like the lottery winner. Both are determined by one ticket (the outcome) being from a bowlful (the full range of possible outcomes). In each case, outcome is chosen from among the many possibilities. Superior investors are people who have a better sense for what tic in the bowl, and thus for whether it’s worthparticipating in the

other words, while superior investors—like everyone else—don’t know exactly what the futureholds,they do have an above-average understand of futuretendencies. As an aside, I want to add a thought here. Most people thi deal with the futureis by formulating an opinion as to what’s happen, perhaps via a probability distribution.I think there are actua requirements, not one. In addition to an opinion regarding what happen, people should have a view on the likelihood that their prove correct. Some events can be predicted with substantial confidence will a given investment grade bond pay the interest it promises?), some uncertain (will Amazon still be the leaderin online retailing in some are entirely unpredictable (will the stock market go up or month?) It’s my point here that not all predictions should be treate likely to be correct, and thus they shouldn’t be relied on equally. most people are as awareof this as they should be. ∾ A good way to think about the superior investor described aboveis someone whose insight into tendencies permits him to tilt the favor. Let’s say there are 100 balls in a jar, some black and some white. color should you bet will come up? If you don’t know anything about the contents of the jar, betting be just a matter of guessing: uninformed speculation.The situati similar if you know there are 50 black and 50 white. You wisely bet on black as white, but doing either wouldn’t give than a 50:50 chance of being right. Thus betting would be you’re offered odds that are at least even—and unless you’re avoid paying an admission charge (in investing, a commission asked spread) to play. Betting on black or white at even odds very profitable other than if you got lucky, and luck isn’t somet can count on. Betting in the absence of a knowledge edge contents of the jar wouldn’t be dependably profitable. But what if you do have special insight regarding the conten Let’s say you know there are 70 black balls and 30 white. That allow you to win more often than you lose. If you can bet $10 against someone who gives you even odds, you’llwin $10 70% of time and lose $10 only 30% of the time, for an expected profit 10 picks.(Note: these will be the outcomes on average over number of trials, but they are subject to significant variation run due to randomness.)

Of course, your betting partner will only give you even odds black (a) if he doesn’t know the balls are 70% black and 30% (b) if he doesn’t know that you do know. If he knew about the contents of the jar, he would give you only 30:70 on black,and the bet would be back to being profitless. In other words, in order to win at this game more often than you have to have a knowledge advantage. That’s what the superi investor has: he knows more than othersabout the futuretendencies. Yet it’s important to remember what I said earlier: even if probabilities—that is, even if you do have superior insight tendencies—you still don’t know what’s going to happen. Even if ratio of balls in the jar is 70 black to 30 white, you color the next one picked will be. Yes, it’s more likely to be white, but it’ll still be white 30% of the time. Whenthere are as well as black in the jar, and especially when random and forcesare at work when the next ball is chosen, there can be regarding the outcome. But all this being said, there doesn’t have to be certainty in game to be worthplaying. A knowledge advantage regarding the tendencies is enough to createsuccess in the long run. And that brings us to the payoff from understanding cycles. investor doesn’t know much about it: He doesn’t fully understand the nature and importance of cycles He hasn’tbeen around long enough to have lived through many He hasn’tread financial history and thus learned the lessons cycles. He sees the environment primarily in terms of isolated events than taking note of recurring patterns and the reasons behind Most important, he doesn’t understand the significanceof cycles what they can tell him about how to act. The superior investor is attentive to cycles. He takes note of patterns seem to be repeating, gains a sense for wherewe stand in cycles that matter, and knows those thingshave implications for This allows him to make helpful judgments about cycles and wher in them. Specifically: Are we close to the beginning of an upswing, or in the If a particular cycle has been rising for a while, has it we’re now in dangerous territory?

Does investors’ behavior suggest they’re being driven by greed fear? Do they seem appropriately risk-averse or foolishly risk-tolerant? Is the market overheated (and overpriced),or is it frigid (and because of what’s been going on cyclically? Taken together, does our current position in the cycle imply should emphasize defensiveness or aggressiveness? Attention to these elements gives the superior investor an edge tha him to win more often than he loses. He understandsthe tendencies or thus he knows something that othersdon’t about the color of the balls jar. He has a sense for whether the chances of winning excee losing; thus he is able to investmore when they are favorable and they aren’t. Importantly, all these thingscan be assessed on the observations regarding current conditions. As we’ll see in later chap can tell us how to prepare for the futurewithout requiring that predict the future. Remember, wherewe stand in the various cycles has a strong the odds. For example, as we’ll see in later chapters, opportunities investment gains improve when: the economy and company profits are more likely to swingupwar down, investor psychology is sober ratherthan buoyant, investors are conscious of risk or—even better—overly concerned risk, and market priceshaven’t moved too high. Thereare cycles in all these things(and more), and knowing wh stand within them can help us tilt the odds in our favor.In short movement through the cycle repositions the probability distribution governing futureevents. Perhaps I should illustrate with regard to inves Whenour position in the various cycles is neutral, the ou “normal.”

Whenthe cycles are positioned propitiously, the probability distribut shifts to the right, such that the outlook for returns is now tilted Our favorable position in the cycles makes gains more likely and so. But when the cycles are at dangerous extremes, the odds are ag meaning the likelihoods are less good. There’s less chance of gain chance of loss.

The same is true when our position changes in only a singlecy example, regardless of what’s going on with regard to the econ company profits (that is, as the academics say, ceteris paribus thingsbeing equal”), the outlook for returns will be better when inves depressed and fearful (and thus allow asset pricesto fall) and worsewhen they’re euphoric and greedy (and drive pricesupward). The odds change as our position in the cycles changes. If our investment stance as these thingschange, we’re being passive regar cycles; in other words, we’re ignoring the chance to tilt the But if we apply some insight regarding cycles, we can increase place them on more aggressive investmentswhen the odds are in our favor and we can take money off the table and increase our defensiven odds are against us. The student of cycles doesn’t know for a fact what’s go —anymore than someone with insight regarding the balls in the jar what color ball will come out next. But both have a knowledge advantage regarding what’s likely. The student’s knowledge of cycles and for wherewe stand at a point in time can make a big contributio that must be present in order for an investor to achieve super ball-chooser who knows the ratio is 70:30 has an advantage. So investor who knows better than otherswherewe stand in the cycle.It’s purpose of this book to help you become that person. In that interest, I’ll describe a number of cyclical processe take place in real time. The oscillations mightseem extreme, and in fact may be, since they’re chosen from the experience of a half-centur point. And they may give the impression that the events under discussion were compressed in time, whereas in truth they took months and years

develop. But these examples are real, and I hope they’ll make my clear.

II THE NATURE OF CYCLES Most people think of cycles in terms of a series of events. people understand that these events regularly follow each other in usual sequence: upswings are followed by downswings, and then eventually by new upswings. But to have a full understanding o cycles, that’s not enough. The events in the life of a c be viewed merely as each being followed by the next, but—m more importantly—as each causing the next. When I meet with Oaktree clients, they almost always ask them make sense of what’s going on in the worldor in the mark usually want to know about one particular cycle or another and wher stand in it. I invariably pull out a sheet of paper and make a discussion. There’s usually a line that stretches from lower left to upper rig line fluctuates up and down around it. Together they look like this.

WhenI started to organize for the task of writing this bo my Oaktree bag and founda large number of such drawings. I them in the course of describing several different phenomena,and annotated differently. But each one related to a cycle worthy of The chapters in this book will generally be devoted to those cycli phenomena. Before moving aheadwith my discussion of cycles, I want to something I mentioned in The Most Important Thing. I confess between discussing the ups and downs of cycles and the sideoscillations of pendulums, applying the cycle label to some phenomena and (as seen in chapter VII) the pendulum label to others(usually those to psychology). Sometimes I’ll talk about a given phenomenon as sometimes as a pendulum. But when pressed, I find it hard to between the two or to say why one gets one label and not the I tend to think about thingsvisually, so perhaps I can use an describe the connection between cycles and pendulums. As I will length later, cycles oscillate around a midpoint (or a secul pendulums hang over a midpoint (or norm)and swingback and forth from there. But if you take the hang-point of the pendulum, turn it on drag it from left to right as it oscillates, what do you get? A Therereally is no fundamental difference. I’ll even admit that a pe is little more than a special case of a cycle,or perhaps just make reference to particular cycles. My reasons for referring to as cycles and othersas pendulums are clear to me. I hope they clear to you as well. Or, at minimum, I hope my use of the detract from what you take from this book. The bottom line is that, in the worldinvestors inhabit, cycles and pendulums swingback and forth. Cycles and pendulum swings come many formsand relate to a wide variety of phenomena,but the unde reasons for them—and the patterns they produce—have a lot in they tend to be somewhat consistent over time. Or as Mark Twain to have said (although there’s no evidence he actually said it), doesn’t repeatitself, but it does rhyme.” Whether Twain said it or not, that sentence sums up a lot is about. Cycles vary in terms of reasons and details, and but the ups and downs (and the reasons for them)will occur forev producing changes in the investment environment—and thus in the that’s calledfor. The central line in my drawings constitutes a midpoint around cycle oscillates. It sometimes has an underlying direction or secular (“secular” as in “of or relating to a long term of indefinite Webster’s New Collegiate Dictionary),and that’s usually upward. So, over

time and in the long run, economies tend to grow, companies’ profits increase and (largely because of those things) markets tend to these developments were scientific or wholly natural, physical proce economies, companies and markets mightprogress in a straight line constant rate (at least for a while). But of course, they’re The fact is that the performance of these thingsis heavily inf short run by, among other things, the involvement of people, far from steady. Rather they fluctuate from time to time, often becau thingswe can lump under the broad heading of “psychology.” Thus peop behavior varies. . . certainly as the environment varies, absence of changes in the environment, too. It’s the oscillation of thingsaround the midpoint or secular tre book is largely about. The oscillation bedevils people who don’t it, are surprised by it or, even worse, partake in and contr I’ve said before, it often presents profit opportunities for those who understand, recognize and take advantage of cyclical phenomena. ∾ It’s clear from looking at my drawings for a few seconds that of cyclical phenomena can be understood as taking place in a identifiable phases: a) recovery from an excessively depressed lower extreme or “low” toward the midpoint, b) the continued swingpast the midpoint toward an upper extrem “high,” c) the attainment of a high,

that these events regularly follow each other in a usual sequence: are followed by downswings, and then eventually by new upswings. In short, the details are unimportantand can be irrelevant. But the are essential, and they absolutely do tend to recur. Understanding that tendency—and being able to spot the recurrences—is one of the most important elements in dealing with cycles. III THE REGULARITY OF CYCLES This effort to explain life through the recognition of patterns—and thus to come up with winning formulas—is complicated, in la because we live in a worldthat is beset by randomnessand in people don’t behave the same from one instance to the next, e when they intend to. The realization that past events were largely affected by these things—andthus that futureevents aren’tfully predictable—is unpleasant, as it makes life less subject to anticipation, rule-makingand rendering safe. In the fall of 2013, in response to something I’d written in Important Thing, I received an email from Nick Train of Lindsell London-based money management firm. Nick took issue with my use word “cycle” to describe phenomena like those I’m discussing here. Nick I had a healthy email colloquy regarding the issue and met for spirited lunch. By the time the main course arrived, it had become clear tha motivated Nick to write was his conviction that for something to be as cyclical, its timing and extenthave to be regular and thus radio cycle or sine wave,for example, rises and falls in a regular, pattern, with the same amplitude, frequency and ending point every time. Dictionary.com defines a cycle in physics as “a complete alt whicha phenomenon attains a maximum and minimum value,retur final value equal to the original one,” and a cycle in mathematics permutationof a set of elements that leaves the original cycli elements unchanged.” In other words, these scientific and mathematic cycles follow patterns so regular that they end up back wher that happens because the timing and path of the fluctuations is same. Score one for Nick. But economies, companies and markets—and certainly investors’ psyches and behavior—are not regular in this way. I asserted at our

Nick eventually agreed, that thingscan be cyclical without exhibiting degree of consistency.It’s all a matter of your definition of Here’s part of what I wrote to Nick to follow up: What I claim is that, usually, thingsrise and fall. Most natural th have a birth/death cycle,and investor psychology has a very pronounced cycle of rising optimism (and price appreciation) followed by rising pessimism (and price declines). You may think that’s simplistic and unhelpful. But one of the main pointsis that when something rises, investors have a tendency to think it’ll never fall (and vice versa). Betting against those tendencies can be v profitable. . . . Little in the world—andcertainly not in the investment world—is regular enough in time to profit from applying a mechanistic p But that doesn’t mean you can’t take advantage of up-and-down cycles. . . . I don’t think fluctuations have to end up back wherethey started be calleda cycle.Manycycles end up higher than they started— is, they are cycles around an underlying secular uptrend—but doesn’t mean they’re not cyclical, or that it’s not desirable to up-cycle and avoid the down-cycle,as opposed to staying on throughout. The Cambridge Dictionary’s definition of a cycle—for use in the non-technical world—is “a groupof events that happen in a one following the other, and are often repeated.” I’m happywith that one; reflects the sense in whichI think about cycles and oscillations in ∾ WhileI don’t agree with Nick Train’s objection that the irregularity of phenomena I’m discussing disqualifies them from being described as cyclical, there is a great deal to be understood about their irregularity and what learned from it. The most important thing to note here is that, as I said in the the thingsI call cycles do not stem completely—or sometimes at the operation of mechanical,scientific or physical processes. They woul much more dependable and predictable if they did—but much less potentially profitable. (This is because the greatest profits come from seeing than othersdo, and if cycles were totally dependable and predictable would be no such thing as superiority in seeing them.) Some underlying principle (and sometimes not), but much variation is attributable the role of humans in creating cycles. The involvement of

process enables their emotionand psychology-induced tendencies to influence cyclical phenomena.Chance or randomnessalso plays a big some cycles, and human behavior contributes to their existence, too. are a big part of the reason these cycles exist, but also—alon randomness—for their inconsistency and thus their undependability. ∾ We humans have to live in the real world. As described earlie patterns and rules that will permit us to live more easilyand profi Perhaps it started with early man’sexperience with daily and annual He may have learned the hard way that it was unsafe to visit at the time of day when mother lions went there with their cubs. IV THE ECONOMIC CYCLE The output of an economy is the product of hours worked per hour; thus the long-term growth of an economy is determi primarily by fundamental factors like birth rate and the rate o productivity(but also by other changes in society and environment) Thesefactors usually change relatively little from year to year, a only gradually from decade to decade. Thus the average rate o growth is rathersteady over long periods of time. Giventhe relative stability of underlying secular growth, one be tempted to expect that the performance of economies w consistent from year to year. However, a number of factors a to variability, causing economic growth—even as it follows th underlying trendline on average—toalso exhibit annual variability. The economic cycle (also known—mostly in the past—as “the busine cycle”) provides much of the foundation for cyclical events in worldand the markets. The more the economy rises, the more likely it companies will expand their profits and stock markets will rise. I’ll briefly here on the factors that influence economic cycles. But I want to make the confession I volunteer whenever I discuss it a proudproclamation?): I’m no economist. I took courses in economics as both an undergraduate and a student. I think about economics. I deal with economics as a profe investor. And I consider myself to be largely an “economic most decisions for logical reasons based on the relationship between value,risk and potential return. But my thinking about economics is largely on common sense and experience, and I’m sure I’ll write thing with whichmany economists will disagree. (Of course, they also disagree with each other. The workings of economics are quite unclear and impr and thus it’s for good reason that it’s called“the dismal science.”) The main measure of an economy’s output is GDP, or gross do product, the total value of all goodsand services produced for final

economy. It can roughly be viewed as the result of multiplying hours people spendworking by the value of the output produced (Earlier in my careerit was calledgross national product, but that gone out of style. The distinction between the two lies in the treatm foreign manufacturers operating in a given country: GDP includes that country’s output, while GNP does not.) The main questions most people (and certainly most investors) care ab with regard to the economy are whether we’ll have growth or given year, and what the rate of change will be. Both of these are of what I call the short-term economic cycle.(I’ll introduce other considerations shortly.) Whenwe think about U.S. GDP growth in a given year, we usually with an assumption in the range of 2% to 3% or so and then for the specific circumstances. But the starting point for each year’ growth is invariably positive. For example, as last year beganthere of discussion about the rate at whichGDP would grow. The optimists it’d be nearly 3%, and the pessimists thought it mightnot reach about everyone thought it would be positive. The official defin recession is two consecutive quarters of negative growth, and very thought GDP growth would fall into negative territory—last year thereafter. Long-Term Economic Trends Manyinvestors are concerned about year-to-yeareconomic growth: high or low, positive or negative. The developments they’re asking abou term considerations. They’re important, but they’re not everything. In long run their importance fades and long-term considerations become more relevant. As I mentioned early on, most of the cycles that attract inv consist of oscillations around a secular trend or central tende oscillations matter a great deal to companies and markets in the changes in regard to the underlying trendline itself will prove to greater overall significance. The oscillations around the trend will in the long run (admittedly after causing much elation or distress years), but changes in the underlying trend will make the biggest our long-term experience. In January 2009, I wrote a memo entitled “The Long View” this subject. I’m going to quote extensively from it here.

First, I described a number of “salutary secular trends” tha markets had been ridingover the preceding decades. I’ll list them below omit the descriptionsthat accompanied them in the memo: macro environment corporate growth the borrowing mentality popularization of investing investor psychology The developments enumerated aboveconstituted a strong tailwind behind the economy and the markets over the last several d and they produced a long-term secular uptrend. Yet, despite the underlying uptrend, there’s been no straight li economy and markets were punctuated every few years by cyclical bouts of short-term fluctuation. Cycles around the trendline made f frequent ups and downs. Most were relatively small and brief, but in the 1970s, economic stagnation set in, inflation reached 16%, th average stock lost almost half its value in two years,and Busines Week magazine ran a cover story trumpeting “The Deathof Equities” (August 13, 1979). No, my forty years in the market haven’t wine and roses.

From time to time we saw better economies and worse—slowdown and prosperity, recession and recovery. Markets, too, rose and fell. Thesefluctuations were attributable to normal economic cycles and to exogenous developments (such as the oil embargo in 1973 and th emerging market crisis in 1998). The Standard & Poor’s 5 few down years in the period from 1975 to 1999, but none in w lost more than 7.5%.On the upside, however, 16 of those 25 y showed returns above15%, and seven times the annual gain exceed 30%. Despite the ups and downs, investors profited overall, inves became a national pursuit, and Warren Buffett, one of A richest men, got that way by buying common stocks and w companies. Whilethe long-term trend sets the potential ec growth rate, the actuallevel of each year’sGDP will vary relative to whichthe trend dictates . . . largely because of the invol Birth rate may determine the long-term trendsin the number of ho worked, but other factors can introduce variation in the short run. willingness to work is not constant. Theremay be times when conditions discourage people from seeking a place in the workforce, as previ mentioned, and also times when worldevents alter the level of consumptio The most obvious example is the ability of worldevents to discourages economic activity. The sub-prime mortgage crisis and financial institution meltdown that reached its apex with the bankruptcy of Lehm Brothers in September 2008 discouragedconsumers from buying, investors

from providing capital, and companies from building factories and expa workforces. Thesecutbacks occurred even among people who hadn’t jobs, suffered home foreclosure or seen declines in the value of their portfolios. Thesedevelopments quickly affected the overall economy, result was a serious recession lasting from December 2007 to June If the number of workers working and the amount they ea constant, we mightexpect the amount they spendon consumption similarly constant. But it isn’t. Spending fluctuates more than employmen and earnings because of variation in something called“the marginal propensity to consume”: of every additional dollarearned, it determines percentage that will go to consumption. Because this propensity is the short run, consumption can vary independently of income. Earners may choose to spenda higher percentage of their ea consumption because: the daily headlines are favorable; they believe election results presage a stronger economy, or lower taxes; consumer credit has become more readily available; asset appreciation has made them feel richer; or their team won the World Series. The fourthof these factors—the so-called “wealth effect”—is particula noteworthy.Asset owners (a) are probably unlikely to fund consumptio selling their stocks or homes and (b) should recognize that can prove ephemeral and thus aren’ta good reason to alter spending Yet asset appreciation does tend to lead them to spendmore. This phenomenon demonstrates the contributionof psychology to behavior, behavior to short-term economic variation. It’s particularly important in this vein to note the extentto whichec expectations can be self-fulfilling. If people (and companies) belie futurewill be good, they’ll spendmore and investmore . . . and will be good, and vice versa.It’s my belief that most companies concluded that the Crisisof 2008 wouldn’t be followed by a V-shaped recov been the rule in the last few recessions. Thus they declined to expa factories or workforces, and the resulting recovery was modest and the U.S. (and even more anemic elsewhere). Another reason for short-term variation concerns inventories. Business may overestimate the demand for their products in a given perio increase production such that it exceeds the amount they can sell. may hold production constant but encounter surprisinglysoft demand. In either case, more goodswill be produced than sold. The excess will be

to inventories. That, in turn, is likely to cause production in subsequent periods to be adjusted downward until inventories are restored to levels. In this way, additions to and reductions in inventories often short-term ups and downs in economic output. Theseare only a few examples of the factors that can cause the economy to vary in a given quarter or year from the growth output that birth rate and productivitygains mightsuggest. They are the of factors that are not “mechanical” or reliable in nature. from human behavior, and thus they are uncertain and unpredictable. ∾ This leads me to add a few wordsabout economic forecasts. Manyinves predicate their actions on forecasts that they make themselves or obtai economists, banksor the media. And yet I doubt many such forecasts information that’s likely to add value and lead to investment success. (For more extensive discussion of “whatwe don’t know,” see chapter 14 Most Important Thing.) Here’s how I view the foundation for considering this issue: In investing, it’s easy to achieve performance that is equal average investor or a market benchmark. Since it’s easy to be average, real investment success must consis outperforming other investors and the averages. Investment success largely a relative concept, measured on the basis of relativ Simply being right about a coming event isn’t enough to ensure relative performance if everyone holds the same view and as everyone is equally right. Thus success doesn’t lie in being ratherin being more right than others. Similarly, one doesn’t have to be right in order to be succes wrong than others. Success doesn’t come from having a correct forecast, but superior forecast. Can such forecasts be obtained? Most economic forecasts consist of extrapolations of current lev long-term trends. And since the economy usually doesn’t depart those levelsand trends, most extrapolation forecasts turn out to be But those extrapolation forecasts are likely to be commonly shared, reflected in the market pricesfor assets, and thus not generators performance—even when they come true. Here’s how NobelPrize–winning economist Milton Friedman put it:

All these people see the same data, read the same material, and s their time tryingto guess what each other is going to say. [Their forecasts] will always be moderately right—and almost never of m use. The forecasts that are potentially valuable are those that correctly for deviation from long-term trendsand recentlevels. If a forecaster make non-conforming, non-extrapolation prediction that turns out to be correct, outcome is likely to come as a surprise to the other market they scramble to adjusttheir holdings to reflect it, the result is gains for the few who correctly foresaw it. There’s only one catch majordeviations from trend (a) occur infrequentlyand (b) are hard to correctly predict, most unconventional, non-extrapolation forecasts turn out be incorrect, and anyone who invests on their basis is usually belowaverage. So these are the possibilitiesI see with regard to economic for Most economic forecasts are just extrapolations. Extrapolations are usually correct but not valuable. Unconventional forecasts of significant deviation from trend would very valuable if they were correct, but usually they aren’t. forecasts of deviation from trend are incorrect and also not valuab A few forecasts of significant deviation turn out to be correc valuable—leadingtheir authors to be lionized for their acumen—bu hard to know in advance whichwill be the few right ones. Since overall batting average with regard to them is low, uncon forecasts can’t be valuable on balance. Thereare forecasters who famous for a singledramatic correct call, but the majority forecasts weren’t worthfollowing. Taken together, these three conclusions on economic forecasting aren’t very encouraging. Thus it’s not for nothing that John Kenneth Galb “We have two classes of forecasters: those who don’t know—and those who don’t know they don’t know.” Secular changes in long-term economic cycles are hard to pre correctness of forecasts of such changes is hard to assess. The of short-term economic cycles, too, are hard for any one person consistentlypredict better than others. It’s tempting to act on econ predictions, especially since the payoff for correct ones theoretically high. But the difficulty of being able to do so correctly and consi mustn’t be underestimated.

Exogenous—Annual performance can also be influenced by ( man-made events that are not strictly economic, such as th occurrence of war; governmentdecisions to change tax rates o adjusttrade barriers; or changes caused by cartels in th commodities, or (b) natural events that occur without th involvement of people, such as droughts, hurricanes and earthquakes. Long-term economic growth is steady for long periods of subject to change pursuant to long-term cycles. Short-term economic growth follows the long-term trend on averag it oscillates around that trendline from year to year. People try hard to predict annual variation as a source investing profit. And on average they’re close to the truth time. But few people do it right consistently; few do it

than everyone else; and few correctly predict the majordeviations trend. ∾ I often find—miraculously—that just as I’m just about to conclude I’m writing, the perfect example pops up in real life or in Thus, as I was completing the first draft of this chapter on June was reported that a majority of votersin the United Kingdom leave the European Union. V GOVERNMENT INVOLVEMENT WITH THE ECONOMIC CYCLE Extreme economic cyclicality is considered undesirable.Too much strength can kindle inflation and take the economy so high th recession becomes inevitable. Too much weakness, on the other hand, can cause companies’ profits to fall and can cost people their jo Thus it is part of the job of central bankers and Treasur manage cycles. Since cycles produce ups and downs that can be excessive, for dealing with them are counter-cyclical and applied with a c their own—ideally inverse to the economic cycle itself. However, li everything else involving cycles, managing them is far from easy. I weren’t, we wouldn’t see the extremes we do. In most of the world, capitalism and free markets are accepted constituting the best system for allocating economic resources and encouraging economic output. Nations have tried other systems, such socialism and communism, but in many cases they have either switched wholesale to or adopted aspects of free markets. Despite the widespread acceptance of the free-market system, markets rarelyleft entirely free. Government involvement takes many forms, from the enactment and enforcement of laws and regulations to direc participation in the economy through entities like the U.S.’s agencies. Perhaps the most important form of governmentinvolvement, however, comes in the attempts of central banksand national control and affect the ups and downs of economic cycles. Central Banks

Over the centuries, significant power and responsibility has been veste central bankssuch as the U.S. Federal Reserve Bank.Whereas in their primary roles may have been to issue currency and exchange it request for gold or silver, today central banksare concerned prim managing economic cycles. Early on, many central banksissued currency. As time passed an bankstook responsibility for cycles, their primary concern usually with inflation. In particular, the worldwent through periods of hype with inflation running in the hundreds of percent per year, as Germany during the post–World War I Weimar Republic. Thus centr turned to managing inflation. The goal wasn’t to eliminate inflat is accepted as (a) having a variety of salutary aspects unavoidable—but to control it. The reasons for inflation are somewhat mysterious and, like many oth processes described here, unreliable and sporadic. Sometimes a given set circumstances will give rise to inflation, and at other times the same circumstances will lead to more or less inflation, or to none at general, inflation is viewed as a result of a strong upwa economic cycle. Whenthe demand for goodsincreases relative to the supply, “demand-pull” inflation. Wheninputsto production such as labor and raw materials increase price, there can be “cost-push” inflation. Finally, when the value of an importing country’s currency declin relative to that of an exporting country, the cost of the can rise in the importing country. The cost of goodscan escalate for any of these reasons. That’s But, as I just said, sometimes these events can occur without an accompanying accelerationof inflation. And sometimes inflation can incre without these thingsbeing present. Thereis a large psychological comp that influences all of this. Since inflation results from economic strength, the efforts of ce bankers to control it amount to tryingto take some of the economy. They can include reducing the money supply, raising and selling securities. Whenthe private sectorpurchases securities from central bank, money is taken out of circulation; this tends to reduc demand for goodsand thus discourages inflation. Central bankers who strongly dedicated to keeping inflation under control are called“haw They tend to do the thingslisted abovesooner and to a greater

The problem, of course, is that actions of this kind are an They can accomplish the goal of keeping inflation under control, but restrain the growth of the economy, with effects that can be beneficial. The issue is complicatedby the fact that in the last few decades, central bankshave been given a second responsibility. In addition controlling inflation, they are expected to support employment, and, course, employment does better when the economy is stronger. So banksencourage this through stimulative actions such as increasing the money supply, decreasing interest rates, and injecting liquidity into economy by buying securities—as in the recentprogram of “qua easing.” Central bankers who focus strongly on encouraging empl lean toward these actions are called“doves.” The bottom line is that most central bankers have two jobs: to inflation, whichrequires restraining the growth of the economy, and support employment, whichcalls for stimulating economic growth. In words, their dual responsibilities are in opposition to each other, and their job requires a delicate balancing act. We have discussed the fact that the economy is cyclical, growing at some times and weakly (or contracting)at others. An economic tends to encourage employment but can cause inflation to accelerate. Stagnation or contraction, on the other hand, discourages inflation but can into employment. So the job of the central banker is to counter-cyclically: that is, to limit the extentof cycles, slowing in times of prosperity in order to keep inflation under control, and the economy during slowdowns to support employment. But just as the investor’s insight into cycles is limited an the central banker’s. His two tasks—stimulating the economy and it—obviously can’t both be done at once. Is it time for stimulus Whichever one is chosen, how much of it? If interest rates are have been since the Global Financial Crisis, in order to provide economic growth is weak (also as it has been), can rates be an increase in inflation without choking off the economy’s tepid cycles are challenging for investors to understand and predict, they easierfor central bankers to manage. Governments Governments have a greater variety of responsibilities than centr only a small portion of whichare related to the economic matte

central banks, they also are charged with stimulating the economy appropriate,albeit not directly with controlling inflation. In their work with the economy, treasuries, too, are concerned with regulating the cycle:not fast and not too slow. Governments’ main tools for managing the economic cycle are fiscal, defined as being concerned primarily with taxing and spending. Thus governments want to stimulate their countries’ economies, they can cut increase governmentspending and even distribute stimulus checks, maki more money available for spending and investment. On the other hand they think economies are growing so fast as to be at risk of setting the scene for a resulting slowdown—governments can incre or cut spending, reducing demand in their economies and thereby economic activity. The ultimate topic under this heading concerns national deficits. In distant past, most governments ran balanced budgets. In short, they able to spendmore money than they brought in through taxes (or But then the concept of national debt arose,and the ability to introduced the potential for deficits: that is, for governments to than they take in. I seem to remember from my youth that there was activedebate reg the propriety of countries having national debt, but we no longe resistance on that subject. It is generally accepted that countries money, although questions do arise from time to time about how much prudent. The answer generally seemsto be “not too much more than now.” The economic theory propoundedby John Maynard Keynes in the dwelled heavily on the role of governments vis-à-vis cycles. economics focuses on the role of aggregate demand in determinin of GDP, in contrast with earlier approaches that emphasized the role supply of goods. Keynes said governments should manage cycle by influencing demand. This, in turn, could be accomplished throu the use of fiscal tools, including deficits. Keynes urged governments to aid a weak economy by stimulati by running deficits. Whena government’s outgo—its spending—excee income—primarily from taxes—on balance it puts funds into the econ This encourages buying and investing. Deficits are stimulative, and thus Keynes considered them helpful in dealing with a weak economy. On the other hand, when economies are strong, Keynes said governm should run surpluses, spending less than they take in. This removes from the economy, discouraging spending and investment. Surpluses are contractionary and thus an appropriate response to booms. However, of surpluses to cool a thriving economy is little seen these days

to be a wet blanket when the party is going strong. And spend you bring in attracts fewer votes than do generous spending programs. surpluses have become as rare as buggy whips. ∾ Extreme economic cyclicality is considered undesirable.Too much strength can kindle inflation and take the economy so high that a reces inevitable. Too much weakness, on the other hand, can cause companies’ profits to fall and cost people their jobs. Thus it is part of the job of central bankers and Treasury manage cycles through the techniques described above. Since cycle ups and downs that can be excessive, the tools for dealing with counter-cyclical and applied with a cycle of their own—ideally inver economic cycle itself. However, like everything else involving cycles—such as knowing wh we are and what to do about them—managing them is far from easy. weren’t, we wouldn’t see the extremes we do.

VI THE CYCLE IN PROFITS The process that determines a company’s profits is complex a multivariate. The economic cycle has a profound effect on some companies’ sales but less on others. Largely because of differen operating and financial leverage, a given percentage change in s has a much greater impact on profits for some companies th others. These days, as I said earlier, the normal growth rate for U be about 2–3%per year. Growth mightcome in at 1% or so in or hit 4% or 5% in boom times (or during a recovery from annualized growth rate mighteven turn negative by a couple of tough times,and if it stays negative for two successive quarters, be recession. So there are fluctuations, but they are moderate: annual U.S. GDP almost always falls between 5% and minus2%, and even extremes are seen only once every decade at most. Does that mean companies’ profits also are stablefrom year to year? Fa from it. Profits can gain much more than 5% in good times and decli more than 2% in bad. They, too, follow a cycle—one that is influ economic cycle,but that rises and falls much more than the economy as whole. So profits are more volatile than GDP. The question is factors cause the profit cycle to perform differently from the economic First, the ups and downs of the economy absolutely are very im determiningthe rise and fall of corporate profits. More GDP means—m than anything else—more consumption, and thus stronger demand for That, in turn, means greater volumes sold and higher selling work and higher wages, and thus still more consumption. All those together mean increased revenues for businesses. By definition, the collective sales of all businesses are one and the GDP, and they reflect the same rate of change. But that doesn’t companies follow the same pattern.

Sales are responsive to the economic cycle in some industries, and in they aren’t. And some respond a lot, while othersrespond just a Sales of industrial raw materials and componentsare directly respon to the economic cycle.Whenbusiness collectively increases its output that is, when GDP expands—ittakes more chemicals, metals, plastic, energy, wire and semiconductors to do so, and vice versa. On the other hand, everyday necessities like food, beverages and medicine aren’thighly responsive to the economic cycle.People generally consume them regardless of what’s going on in the (But demand isn’t absolutely constant: people trade down in recess —buying cheaper food and eatingat home ratherthan in restaurants— and they trade up in times of prosperity. And, sadly,people who struggling financially may cut down even on their consumption of “necessities” when forced to choose between food, medications payments.) Demand for low-cost consumer items (like everyday clothing, newspapers and digital downloads) isn’t very volatile, while demand luxury goodsand vacation trips may be. Purchases of big-ticket “durable goods”—things like cars and homes individuals and trucksand factory equipment for businesses—are highly responsive to the economic cycle.First, the fact that they’re durabl means they last a long time, so replacementcan be deferred economic weakness. Second, because they cost a lot, they’re affordin bad times and easierto affordgood times.And third, businesses generally need more of them when business is good and less when isn’t. Thesethingsmake the demand for durables highly responsive the economic cycle. Demand for everyday services generally isn’t volatile. The meaning of more immediately obvious from the British word for it: gearing. First, businesses are subject to operating leverage. Profits equal rev minuscosts (or expenses). Revenues are the result of sales, and we know sales fluctuate for a large number of reasons. So do costs, and of costs fluctuate in different ways, particularly in response to chan sales. Most businesses have some costs that are fixed, some that are semi-fixed, and some that are variable. For example, take a taxicab company: It has its headquarters in an officebuilding, but when ridersh a bit, it doesn’t need to add more officespace. This is fixed cost. It has a fleet of taxis. The current fleet can accommodate increase in ridership, but if trips increase enough, it may additional cabs. Thus the expense for cabs is semi-fixed. Its cabs are powered by gasoline. If an increase in busine cabs to go x% more miles,their consumption of gasoline is increase by that same x%. For the taxi company, then, the cost gasoline is variable. The sum of the abovemeans that if the company’s ridership (an revenues) increases by 20%, its spending on officefacilities won’tincre its spending on cabs probably won’tincrease initially, but may later; spending on gasoline will increase immediately and proportionately. taxicab company’s total costs will increase with an increase in rider usually less than its revenues. This will cause its profit margin to meaning the increase in operating profits will be considerably increase in sales: that’s operating leverage. In general, it’s higher

companies for whom a largerpercentage of costs are fixed and lowe ones whose costs are more variable. Operating leverage is great for companies when the economy does we sales rise. But when the opposite happens, it’s less good: profits can than sales, and if conditions are bad enough, profits can turn into Companies can, however, take actions to limit the effect on profits decline. If the company’s $30,000 capital has been sourced entirely through equity, meaning it hasn’tborro its capital and doesn’t have to make any interest payments, that flow through to the company’s net income—the “bottom line”—and would fall by 33%. The economic cycle the backdrop for changes in companies’ sales and profits, but the deviation from the cycle-based expectation is vast. Idiosyncratic developments are the main reason. I’ll take a moment here to deal with the subject of technolo devote a separate chapter to it). “Disrupt” is the word of ability of new technologies to disrupt traditional industries can competitionand dismantle the incumbents’ profit margins. Take, for exam the newspaper industry. As recently as the 1990s: Newspaperswere considered an indispensable source of information. Most people bought a newspaper daily—or maybe one on work and another on the way home—and the cost was small. Even if you bought the newspaper on Monday, you still had another on Tuesday; there was no “shelflife” or protracted useful Newspaperswere one of the very few ways local businesses like movie theaters and used-car dealers could reach their customers, and a newspaper from one city generally couldn’t compete for local advertising from another. Competition came primarily from other newspapers,television and radio. Once a newspaper was strongly established in a city, howev would be hard to displace—thus newspapers were viewed as with strong “moats.” Because this combination of factors was seen as making ne position largely impregnable, newspaper company stocks were thoug “defensive,” benefiting from highly stablerevenues and profits. Who would have thought that the Internet and other formsof on communication would significantly impact the fortunes of news than twenty years? Technologies are born, they prosper, and then they are replaced by ones. The innovation of a few years ago can be supplanted more rapid ever these days, and the list of industries that are perceived as being to disruption seemsto shrink every day. Thirty or forty years ago, it seemed as if the worldwas a provided a relatively unchanging backdrop for life, and economic developments—including cycles—played out against that unchanging backdrop. Today, largely because of technological developments socialand cultural), nothing seemsunchanging.In fact, much appears to changing too fast for most of us to keep up with it.

VII THE PENDULUM OF INVESTOR PSYCHOLOGY In business, financial and market cycles, most excesses on th —andthe inevitable reactions to the downside, whichalso tend to overshoot—are the result of exaggeratedswings of the pendulum o psychology.Thus understanding and being alert to excessive swings is an entry-level requirementfor avoiding harm from cyclical extremes, and hopefully for profiting from them. So far we have discussed the economic cycle,governments’ efforts influence the economic cycle,and the profit cycle.To a great extentthese thingsprovide the backdrop or environment for investing. And they to be exogenous to investing—independent processes that operate on own. (Psychology and emot certainly different elements, but I see no meaningful way to distin between the two in terms of their effect on the investment environmen First, swings in emotion/psychology strongly influence the economi corporate profit cycles, as indicated. And second, they play a very part in causing ups and downs in the investment world—especiall short run. As I noted in chapter I, there is no fundamental distinctio and pendulum swings. In fact, I could have made life easierfor all titlingthis chapter “The Cyclein Psychology” and fittingthis phenomeno within a consistent nomenclature. But, for some unspecified reason, introduced the notion of the “pendulum”in emotion/psychology in second memo to clients, “First Quarter Performance” (April1991 I haven’t founda reason to distance myself from it in going to continue to refer to it here. To introduce the pendulum, I’m going to borrow from what I wr 1991:

The mood swings of the securities markets resemble the movem a pendulum. Although the midpoint of its arc best describes th location of the pendulum “on average,” it actually spends v its time there. Instead, it is almost always swinging toward from the extremes of its arc. But whenever the pendulum is n either extreme, it is inevitable that it will move back toward th midpoint sooner or later. In fact, it is the movement toward extreme itself that supplies the energy for the swingback. Investment markets make the same pendulum-like swing: between euphoria and depression, between celebrating positive developments and obsessing over negatives, and thus between being overpriced and underpriced. This oscillation is one of the most dependable features of the investment world, and investor psychology seemsto spendmuch more time at the extremes than it does at a “happy medium I returned to the topic in “It’s All Good” (July 2007). Be make a new observation,I listed a half-dozen additional elements in pendulum swings are seen: between greed and fear, between optimism and pessimism, between risk tolerance and risk aversion, between credence and skepticism, between faith in value in the futureand insistence of concrete value in the present, and between urgency to buy and panic to sell. I find particularly interesting the degree to whichthe polarities li aboveare interrelated.Whena market has been rising strongly for a while, we invariably see all nine of the elements listed first. A the market’s been declining, we see all of the elements listed s Rarely do we see a blend of the two sets, given that the c each are causally related, with one giving rise to the next. A lot of what I wrote then about the pendulum correspondsdirectly what I wrote about cycles back in chapter I. There’s a swin extreme or the other;then the attainment of an extreme that can’t exceeded; and then a swingback toward the midpoint, fueled by

of momentum.It mightbe said that the pendulum also tends to regre the mean or midpoint, but—like most cycles—it usually overshoots and continues toward the extreme opposite from whence it came. ∾ Why is the pendulum important? In essence, the too-strong upward downward swings of the cycles I’m covering in this book large —andrepresent—psychological excesses in action. The trendline rate of growth in economic output and corpor moderate, and when participants’ pro-cyclical decisions cause growth be abnormally rapid (otherthan in times of recovery from recession), this usually represents a too-optimistic expansion from whichthere be a retreat. Likewise, it seemsrational that, in the long run, stocks overal provide returns in line with the sum of their dividends plus growth in corporate profits, or something in the mid-to-high digits. Whenthey returnmuch more than that for a while, that likely to prove to have been excessive—borrowing from the futureand thus rendering stocks risky—meaning a downward correction is order. In business, financial and market cycles, most excesses on the the inevitable reactions to the downside, whichalso tend to overshoot— the result of exaggeratedswings of the pendulum of psychology.Thus understanding and being alert to excessive swings is an entry-level requirementfor avoiding harm from cyclical extremes, and hopefully for profiting from them. The norms in terms of growth and appreciation are in so and “healthy.” And if the participants built their behavior around those —instead of occasionally building up hopesfor more and thus settin stage for eventual moves toward less—the worldwould be a tempestuous, and less-error-prone place.But that’s not the nature of I touched on the inapplicability of norms in “The Happy July 2004, but now with data brought up to date through 2016): Putting it all together, fluctuations in attitudes and behavior c to make the stock market the ultimate pendulum. In my 47 f calendar years in the investment business, starting with 1970, the annual returns on the S&P 500 have swung from plus 37% to 37%. Averaging out good years and bad years,the long-run returnis

usually statedas 10% or so. Everyone’s been happywith that typical performance and would love more of the same. But remember, a swinging pendulum may be at its midpoint average,” but it actually spends very little time there. The same is of financial market performance. Here’s a fun question ( illustration): for how many of the 47 years from 1970 through 2 was the annual returnon the S&P 500 within 2% of “norma is, between 8% and 12%?
