They’re largely attributable to the mood swings of investors. Lastly, the times when returnis at the extremes aren’trandomly distributed over the years.Rather they’re clustered, due to the f investors’ psychological swings tend to persist for a while— paraphrase Herb Stein, they tend to continue until they stop. Most of those 13 extreme up or down years were within a year or tw another year of similarly extreme performance in the same d How about an example of the pendulum in action? One of the most time-honored market adages says that “markets between greed and fear.” There’s a fundamental reason for this: people fluctuate between greed and fear. In other words, sometimes feel positive and expect good things, and when that’s the case, they greedy and fixate on making money. Their greed causes them to make investments, and their bidding causes markets to rise and appreciate. But at other times,they feel less good and their expectations turn ne In that case, fear takes over. Rather than enthuse about making mone worryabout losingit. This causes them to shrink from buying—eliminat the upward impetus beneath asset prices—andperhaps to sell, push down. Whenthey’re in “fear mode,” people’s emotions bring nega to bear on the markets. Here’s part of the discussion of the swingbetween greed and fea “The Happy Medium” (July 2004):

WhenI was a rookie analyst, we heard all the time that “ market is driven by greed and fear.” Whenthe market environ in healthy balance, a tug-of-war takes place between optimists in on making money and pessimists seeking to avoid losses. T want to buy stocks, even if they have to pay a price a b yesterday’s close, and the latter want to sell them, even if it’s on a downtick. Whenthe market doesn’t go anyplace, it’s because the senti behind this tug-of-war is evenly divided, and the people—or f —on the two ends of the rope carry roughly equal weight. The optimists may prevail for a while, but as securities are b become more highly priced, and then the pessimists gain sway a sell them down. . . . It didn’ttake long in my early days, however, for me to reali often the market is driven by greed or fear. At the times th count, large numbers of people leave one end of the rope f Eitherthe greedy or the fearful predominate, and they move th market dramatically. Whenthere’s only greed and no fear, for example, everyone wantsto buy, no one wantsto sell, and few p can think of reasons why pricesshouldn’t rise. And so they do—of in leaps and bounds and with no apparent governor. Clearly that’s what happened to tech stocks in 1999. Greedwas dominant characteristic of that market. Thosewho weren’t participating were forced to watcheveryone else get rich. “Pruden investors” were rewarded with a feeling of stupidity. The buyers moving that market felt no fear. “There’s a new paradigm,” w battle cry, “get on board before you miss the boat. And by the w the price I’m buying at can’t be excessive, because the market always efficient.” Everyone perceived a virtuous cycle in favor o tech stocks to whichthere could be no end. But eventually something changes. Eithera stumbling block materializes, or a prominent company reports a problem, o exogenous factorintrudes. Pricescan also fall under their own weight or based on a downturn in psychology with no obvious cause. Certainly no one I know can say exactly what it was that b tech-stock bubble in 2000. But somehow the greed evaporated and fear took over. “Buy before you miss out” was replaced by “Sell before it goes to zero.” And thus fear comes into the ascendancy.People don’t worryabou missing opportunities; they worryabout losingmoney. Irrational exuberance is replaced by excessive caution. Whereas in 1999 p the-sky forecasts for a decade out were embraced warmly, in

investors chastened by the corporate scandals said, “I’ll never trust management again” and “Howcan I be sure any financial s are accurate?” Thus almost no one wanted to buy the bondso scandal-plagued companies, for example, and they sunk to giveaway prices. It’s from the extremes of the cycle of fear and greed th the greatest investment profits, as distressed debt demonstrated in 2003. “Greed/fear” is the most obvious psychological or emotional co along whichinvestors swing, and in many ways the most illustrative. Wha are some of the other key emotional or psychological swings? Most a manner similar to the greed/fear pendulum, and usually this coincidence. The various parameters are interrelated.Here are a few examples. Underlying the swingbetween greed and fear is the swingbetween euphoria and depression. As previously described, for example, it may simply that positive events give rise to greed. Rather, posit encourage euphoria, whichabets greed (and vice versa for negative events, depression and fear). Euphoria and depression are the foundation emotions that give rise to the swings that follow. Euphoric investors may be excited about current developments an that may arise in the future, and this may accentuate their fixation expectation of—profit. On the other hand, depressed investors are unlikely feel positive enough to be greedy. If you think about it, euph inconsistentwith fear, and depression is inconsistentwith greed. In a similar vein, investors also swingbetween optimism and pe Positive events generally give rise to expectations of further and positive outcomes . . . a state better known as optim underlie greed; it makes no sense to think people can be forward to investwhen their expectations are negative. Clearly, optim pessimism encourage other emotions and influence behavior. ∾ The next phenomenon I want to touch on is the tendency of between credulousness and skepticism, and to fluctuate between being entranced by the possibility of profit in the futureand insistent on value in the here and now. Sometimes—usually when thingsare going well in the worldand asset pricesare rising—investors become willing to swallow favorable storie regarding futuredevelopments, buy into appreciated assets, and shoulder elevated risk. But when thingstake a turn for the worse, they beco likely to reject even reasonable projections and decline to buy, largely

because pricesare down (even though that increases the likelihood that are cheap). Some investors spendtheir time working hard to quantify this year’s earnings and the growth thereafter. Others strive to value real a intellectual property and business advantages (and predict what others will pay for them). Still otherstry to deduce the value implica mergers and acquisitions, balance sheet restructurings and private public transactions. In all of these ways and many more, it’s th those in the investment business to predict the futureand put a on it. Let me give you an example. In 2000–01, our distressed debt invested a few hundred million dollars in bankrupt telecom companies. In each case, the purchase price implied a value for th company that was a small fraction of the amounts that had b invested in hard assetssuch as switching gear or fiber-optic cable.I we could resell the equipment for a higher percentage of its c we had paid, the investment would be profitable. The first sale went well, and we made a quick 50%. But soon thereafter, people stopped showing up to bid on these assets. the party to whom we had sold the first company thought h bargain, in later instances the possible buyers shied away from a that were turning out to be in heavyoversupply. And that brings my point. In 1999, investors accepted at face value their telecom companies’ rosy predictions of the future, and they were willing to pay handily for that potential. But in 2001, they saw the potenti largely empty and wouldn’t pay a dime for it, given that th capacity vastlyexceeded its current needs and no one could imagin the excess being absorbed in their lifetime. This cycle in investo willingness to value the futureis one of the most powerful cycles exists. A simple metaphor relating to real estate helped me to this phenomenon: What’s an empty building worth? An e building (a) has a replacementvalue,of course, but it (b) th no revenues and (c) costs money to own, in the form of ta insurance, minimum maintenance, interest payments and opportunity costs. In other words, it’s a cash drain. Wheninvestors are in a pessimistic mood and can’t see more than a few years out, they can only think about the negative cash flows and are unable to imagin time when the building will be rented and profitable. But when th mood turns upward and interest in futurepotential runs high, investo

envision it full of tenants, throwing off vast amounts of c salable at a fancy price. Fluctuation in investors’ willingness to ascribe value to possible futuredevelopments represents a variation on the full-or-empty c Its swings are enormously powerful and mustn’t be underestimate (“TheHappy Medium,” July 2004) ∾ The superior investor is mature, rational, analytical, objective and unemotional. Thus he performs a thorough analysis of investment fundamentals and the investment environment. He calculates the intrin value of each potential investment asset. And he buys when any discount the price from the current intrinsic value,plus any potential increases intrinsic value in the future, together suggest that buying at a good idea. In order to be able to do all the above, the superior inv appropriate balance between fear (which is shorthand for risk avers for loss, and respect for uncertainty and randomness) and greed (a.k.a aspiration, aggressiveness and acquisitiveness). All people feel emotions, the superior investor keeps these conflicting elements in balance. The presence of the two offsetting forcesleads to responsible,wise and even keeled behavior. But the important pointsare these: Few people are always even-keeledand unemotional. For this reason, few investors are capable of staking out position that balances greed and fear—and staying there—as moreless-positive developments arise. To the contrary, most investors swingbetween being greedy when they’re optimistic and fearful when they’re pessimistic. Most swingto those positions at the wrong time—becoming greedi after the emergence of positive developments has caused prices become elevated, and becoming more fearful after negative events caused pricesto become depressed. Here’s some of what I wrote about the swingof psychology in “O Couch” (January 2016): Thereare many more ways in whichnon-objective, non-rational quirks commonly affect behavior. As Carol Tavris pointed out in May 15, 2015 Wall StreetJournal review of Prof. Richard Thaler’ book Misbehaving: The Making of Behavioral Economics (2015):

As a socialpsychologist, I have long been amused by economists and their curiously delusional notion of the “rational man.”Rational? Where do these folks live? Even 50 years ago, experimental studies were demonstrating that people stay with clearly wrong decisions ratherthan change them, throwgood money after bad, justify failed predictions ratherthan admit they were wrong, and resist,distort or actively reject information that disputes their beliefs. The difficulty of understanding events, their significanceand their potential ramifications comes in good part from the kinks in in psyches, and it contributes to—and feeds back to exacerbate— investors’ responses. Thus investors tend to emphasize just the positives or the negatives much more often than they take a balance objective approach. And they tend to become optimistic and eager to buy when good news, positively interpreted, has forced pricesup . . and vice versa.All of this is obvious (especially in retrospect). T equally obviously, understanding and dealing with it presents a potential way to improve results. The basic point is that psychology does swing, and most people’s be swings with it. The fluctuation between greed and fear is typical of the psychological pendulum. In fact, it explains not only the most investors, but also—taking investors collectively—the behavior of markets. Markets move upward when events are positive and psych turns up, and they fall when events are negative and psychology turns The pendulum spends only a little of the time at the midpoint Rather, the pendulum is usually swinging toward one extreme first recovering from one psychological extreme—either too high or too —andthen continuing toward the other. The superior investor resists psychological excesses and thus ref participate in these swings. The vast majority of the highly super I know are unemotional by nature. In fact, I believe their is one of the great contributorsto their success. This is one of my most persistent observations and—in a rel of the questions I’m most often asked is whether people can learn unemotional. My answer is “yes and no.” I think it’s possible on the lookout for potential emotional influences and to try to effect. But I also think people who are inherently unemotional much easier. A lack of emotionality is a gift (in investing, not in other areas, like marriage). It’s not my point that emotional peop

can’t be good investors, but it will require a great deal of self-a self-restraint. ∾ In addition to the interrelatedness of the various emotional swin on the last few pages, it’s also important to note the causal phenomena.Just as positive events give rise to euphoria, and euph rise to optimism, and optimism abets an increase in greed, swin of all these elements cause the way investors perceive thingsto fluctu between rosy and dark. Investors’ perception of events is colored swings along the various emotional or psychological arcs. And this perception feeds back, creating more euphoria, optimism and greed. Here’s how I put it in “On the Couch” (January 2016): One of the most significant factors keeping investors from reachin appropriate conclusions is their tendency to assessthe worldwith emotionalism ratherthan objectivity. Their failings take two primary forms: selective perception and skewed interpretation. In other w sometimes they take note of only positive events and ignore th negative ones, and sometimes the opposite is true. And sometimes they view events in a positive light, and sometimes it’s negativ rarelyare their perceptions and interpretations balanced and neutral. Ever since the events of August 2015 in China, I’ve repea foundmyself harking back to one of the oldestcartoons in m and still one of the very best:

“Everything that was good for the market yesterday is no good f © BERNARD SCHOENBAUM The bottom line is that investor psychology rarelygives equal weight to both favorable and unfavorabledevelopments. “On Wall Street today, news of lower interest rates sent the stock market up, but that these rates would be inflationary sent the market down, until the realization stimulate the sluggish economy pushed the market up, before it ultimately overheated economy would lead to a reimposition of higher interest rates.” B © BOB MANKOFF; WWW.BOBMANKOFF.COM It all seemsso obvious: investors rarelymaintain objective, rational, neutral and stablepositions. First they exhibit high levelso optimism, greed, risk tolerance and credulousness, and their resultin behavior causes asset pricesto rise, potential returns to fall, a to increase. Notably, each groupof phenomena tends to happen in unison, and the swingfrom one to the other often g beyond what reason mightcall for. That’s one of the crazy things: in the real world, thing fluctuate between “pretty good”and “not so hot.” But in the w investing, perception often swings from “flawless” to “hopeless.” The pendulum careens from one extreme to the other, spending almost time at “the happymedium” and ratherlittle in the range of reasonableness. First there’s denial, and then there’s capitulation.

The worldis full of positive and negative events, and on mo some of each. And some of the events that occur are ambiguous, havin elements of both good and bad, making them subject to either posit negative interpretation. Take the example of the second cartoon. Low interest rates are because they stimulate business activity and increase the discounted value of futurecash flows. But they’re also bad, since the stronger activity they abet can give rise to inflation and thus signalthe centr that rates should be raised, withdrawing stimulus from the econ Interpretation may not fluctuate between the extremes several times the cartoon suggests. Here’s how investors react to even they’re feeling good about life (which usually means the mark rising): Strong data: economy strengthening—stocks rally Weakdata: Fed likely to ease—stocks rally Data as expected: low volatility—stocks rally Banks make $4 billion: business conditions favorable—stocksrally Banks lose $4 billion: bad news out of the way—stocks Oil spikes: growing global economy contributingto demand—st rally Oil drops: more purchasing power for the consumer—stocks Dollar plunges: great for exporters—stocks rally Dollar strengthens:great for companies that buy from abroad—stocks rally Inflation spikes: will cause assetsto appreciate—stocks rally Inflation drops: improves quality of earnings—stocks rally Of course, the same behavior also applies in the opposite dir psychology is negative and markets have been falling for a while is capable of being interpreted negatively. Strong economic data is likely to make the Fed withdraw stimulus by raising interest rates data is taken to mean companies will have trouble meeting earnings In other words, it’s not the data or events; it’s the interpretati fluctuates with swings in psychology. At the greatest extremes of the pendulum’s swing, a process

appearance of a virtuous circle or a vicious circle. Wheneven predominantly positive and psychology is rosy, negative developments to be overlooked, everything is interpreted favorably, and thingsare often thought to be incapable of taking a turn for the worse. expectation of further advances appears irresistible; past constraints and norms are ignored or rationalized away;and anyone imagining limit the positive futureis dismissed as an old fogey lacking imagination potential for gains comes to be viewed as infinite. Asset price encouraging further optimism. But on the other hand, when thingshave been going badly for months or years and psychology is highly negative, it’s the potential for impr that can be forgotten. Price resulting in further pessimism. The virtuous circle and the vicious circle are both unrealistic exaggera Whilethey’ve been dreamed up many times in the past, they’ve neve out. But that fact doesn’t enable most people to resist them while rampant. Again, the superior investor—who resists external influences, rem emotionallybalanced and acts rationally—perceives both positive and negative events, weighs events objectively and analyzes them dispa But the truth is that sometimes euphoria and optimism cause most inves view thingsmore positively than is warranted, and sometimes depression and pessimism make them see only bad and interpret events with a nega Refusing to do so is one of the keys to successful investing. ∾ For a bullish phase to hold sway, the environment has to b characterized by greed, optimism, exuberance,confidence, credulity, daring, risk tolerance and aggressiveness. Busts are the product of booms, a it’s usually more correct to attribute a bust to the excesse preceding boom than to the specific event that sets off the correcti (“Now What?,” January 2008) Usually, when either set of polar extremes is in the ascendancy fact is readily observable, and thus the implications for investo should be obvious to objective observers. But of course, th the market pendulum to one extreme or the other occurs f

VIII THE CYCLE IN ATTITUDES TOWARD RISK The rational investor is diligent, skeptical and appropriately r averse at all times,but also on the lookout for opportunities potential returnthat more than compensates for risk. That’s the id But in good times,we hear most people say, “Risk? What risk? I don’t see much that could go wrong: look how well thingshave been going. Get me out!” Now that we’vemoved on from considering cycles in the abstra discussing their operation in the investment world, I’m going to provi brief aside regarding the fundamental nature of investing, in order a foundation for the discussion that will follow. Some of this admi be familiar from earlier chapters. What is investing? One way to think of it is as bearing ris profit. Do we know what will happen in the future? Some investors thi —or think they have to act as if they do, because if they don’t their jobs and their clients—or they have been pursuing profit through forecasts for so long that they’ve brainwashed themselves into belie possible to be right about the future(and have become conditioned to their low past success rates). Other investors—the smarter, more self-a ones, I think—understand that the futureisn’t knowable with certainty. may form opinions regarding futureevents, but they don’t bet heavily those opinions will prove correct. Since (a) investing consists of dealing with the futurebut (b) the isn’t knowable, that’s wherethe risk in investing comes from. If futur

were predictable, investing would be easy and profit would be sure. general level of returns mightbe low in that case because so involved; that’s a topic for another day.) But the fact that events predictable introduces risk. Because the events that actually occur may different than those that were predicted, or the market’s reaction to may differ from what was expected, a portfolio may be positioned incor for the futurethat unfolds. Since risk (that is, uncertainty with regard to futuredevelopments, an possibility of bad outcomes) is the primary source of the chall investing, the ability to understand, assessand deal with risk is the the superior investor and an essential—I’m tempted to say the requirementfor investment success. Finally as to foundation, it’s important to recognize that while the investment environment variesover time, at any particular point in time given. My view that risk is the main moving piece in investing makes conclude that at any given point in time, the way investors collectively viewing risk and behaving with regard to it is of overwhelm shaping the investment environment in whichwe find ourselves. Sometimes investors become

risk-averse, and sometimes they relax their risk aversion and become too tolerant. Whenpositive events occur as described in the last chapter an optimism and greed rise, investors tend to become less risk-averse than and less risk-averse than they should be. What are the effects (follo from the list of the functions investors perform on page 106–107)? Since they feel better about the environment and are more optimistic about likely outcomes, they reduce the amount of caution they the investing process. Since they no longer consider investing to be risky, they don’t need for painstaking analysis. They tend to make assumptions that are more generous, and they skepticism with credulousness. They’re willing to make do with a reduced margin of Viewing risk as less worrisome, they no longer demand risk cushyas in the past. They behave less as sticklers, since they’re more attracted to on risky investmentsand less leery of the risk they involve. It is for these reasons that, as you’llsee in the next chapter, financings are completed in the most buoyant economies and financial markets. Good times cause people to become more optimistic, jettison caution, and settle for skimpy risk premiums on risky investments. since they are less pessimistic and less alarmed, they tend to lose intere the safer end of the risk/return continuum. This combination of elem makes the pricesof risky assetsrise relative to safer assets. Thus come as a surprise that more unwise investmentsare made in good in bad. This happens even though the higher priceson risky inves mean the prospective risk premiums offered for making those riskier investmentsare skimpier than they were in more risk-conscious times. The reduced insistence on adequate risk premiums causes the slo capital market line to flatten. Going back to high school that the slope of a line on a graph is the distance traveled per unit change along the horizontal axis. The slope of the capital reflects the amount of incremental potential returnthat is offered incremental risk borne. Thus it is a direct indication of the degre aversion present in the market. In times of obliviousness toward risk—or high risk tolerance—the demand in terms of risk premiums causes the slope of the line the amount of risk compensation to shrink.

The lower slope of the capital market line means, by definitio there’s less of a returnincrement per unit increase in risk. In the payoff for risk-bearingis sub-par. In my opinion, all of the abovefollows logically from direct ob The process is as follows: positive events lead to increased optimism, increased optimism makes people more risk-tolerant, an increase in risk tolerance causes lower risk premiums to demanded, a reduction in demanded risk premiums equates to lower deman returns on risky assets, a reduction of demanded returns on risky assetscauses their rise, and higher pricesmake assetseven riskier (but also attract buying of “momentum investors” who chase rising stocks). It follows from the abovethat risk is high when investors feel ris And risk compensation is at a minimum just when risk is at (meaning risk compensation is most needed). So much for the ration investor!

For me, the bottom line of all of this is that the greatest investment risk is the belief that there is no risk. Widespreadrisk tolera or a high degree of investor comfort with risk—is the great subsequent market declines. But because most investors are following progression described just above, this is rarelyperceived at the time perceiving it—and turning cautious—is most important. ∾ Since the other side of the coin should be obvious, I won’tbelab will take a minute to talk about what happens when the downward the cycle in attitudes toward risk makes investors more risk-averse One of the most important characteristics of psychological cycles extremeness. Cycles swingnot only in directions and degrees that sense,but also in wacky ways and to excess. For example, inves from time to time and say, “Let’s disregard risk. We’ll all get rich.” ardor and excitement cause them to bid pricesto levelsthat are so high— to accept tales that clearly are so unrealistic—that after the fact laughable if the damage done weren’t so great. And after they have committed these sins of excess—and lost a lot money in the resultant downswing toward greater prudence—they themselves for their excesses of greed and credulousness. They wonder they could have behaved so foolishly. They confess that they never really understood the exoticand exciting investment activities in whichthey had engaged. And they vow never to do it again. Just as the inadequacy of their risk aversion allowed them to pu and buy at the top—egged on by the visionof easy money in they couldn’t discern any risk—now they push pricesdown and sell at bottom. Their unpleasant recentexperience convinces them—contrary to what they had thought when everything was going well—that investing is a field in whichthey shouldn’t engage. And, as a consequence, their aversion goes all the way from inadequate to excessive. Giventheir recentpainful experience and the negativity they’ve developed about what lies ahead, they amp up their caution. Since they now associate investing with loss ratherthan profit, their process comes to emphasize the avoidance of further loss prospecting for opportunity. They ensure that their assumptions are conservative enough all potential for disappointment, and they apply extreme skepticism. They find it impossible to identify—even to imagine—investments offer an adequate margin of safety.

Since they see risk everywhere,they consider even the current swolle risk premiums insufficient. They become worrywarts.Just as risk tolerance had positioned them become buyers of overpriced assetsat the highs,now their scream risk aversion makes them sellers—certainly not buyers—at the bottom That’s the point. Under these circumstances, the perception of ris exaggeratedand the slope of the capital market line becomes exces In precisely the reverse of my description of the flat capital higher slope of this one means the expected returnincrement per in risk is unusually generous. This is a risk-averse market, and exaggeratedpayoff for risk-bearing. Thus the reward for bearing risk is greatest at just the moment when—no, rather, just becau absolutely refuseto bear it. As risk attitudes swingfrom high to low, so do opportunities for loss. Wheneverything’s going well and asset pricesare soaring, investors tend to view the futureas rosy, risk as their friend, and profit as achieved. Everyone feels the same, meaning little risk aversion is incorporated in prices, and thus they’re precarious. Investors beco tolerant just when they should increase their risk aversion.

And when events are down, so are investors. They think of the place to lose money, risk as something to be avoided at all depressingly likely. As I described at the end of the last excess of caution that prevails, (a) no one will accept possi incorporate any optimism at all and (b) they likewise cannot coun possibility that an assumption could be “too bad to be true.” Just as risk tolerance is unlimited at the top, it is non-exis This negativity causes pricesto fall to levelsfrom whichlossesare highl unlikely and gains could be enormous. But the sting of the prior decli tends to increase risk aversion and send investors to the sidelines (and thus risk) are at their lowest. ∾ I’d like to provide a real-life example of changing attitudes, of some quantification, taken from “The Happy Medium” (July 2004): The prevalence of risk-tolerance (or risk-obliviousness) in the la 1990swas clear. I personally heard a prominent brokerage house strategist say, “Stocks are overpriced, but not enough to keep th from being a buy.” And we all heard the man on the street say, “ up so much in my 401(k), it wouldn’t bother me if it (Where was that guy two or three years later?) No, those risk-tolerantattitudes will not persist forever. Eventually something will intrude, exposing securities’ imperfections and toohigh prices. Priceswill decline. Investors will like them less at $ than they did at $100. Fear of losingthe remaining $60 will overtak the urge to make back the lost $40. Risk aversion eventually will reassert itself (and usually go to excess). How about some quantification of this cycle? In mid-1998, just before the collapse of Long-Term Capital Management brough investors other than techies to their senses, only $12.5 billion o defaulted bondsyielded more than 20% (one possible threshold for the label “distressed debt”). Because investors weren’t very worried about risk, they demanded ultra-high returns from relatively few nondefaulted bonds; the word “blithe” mightbest describe their attitude But Long-Term’s demise awakened investors to the existence risk, and a year later, the amount of bondsyielding more than 2 had more than tripled to $38.7 billion. By mid-2002, when the corporate scandals held the debt market in a grip of terror, yielders had grown to $105.6 billion, eight and a half ti just four years earlier. Risk aversion had come a long way from inadequate and, as later events showed, had become excessive. B

March 31, 2004, this figurehad fallen 85%, back to just $16.2 billion; risk aversion had subsided (and possibly had become inadequate again). I’m sure that fundamentals didn’tfluctuate anywhere near th degree reflected in prices, yieldsand thus the distressed debt ta usual,reality was greatly exaggeratedby swings in psychology. Wheninvestors in general are too risk-tolerant, security price embody more risk than they do return. Wheninvestors are too r averse, pricescan offer more returnthan risk. The title of the memo cited above—“The Happy Medium”—wa by my mother’s wisdom and her constant reminders that we shou extremes in behavior. Rather we should tend toward the midd things—toward a reasonable balance between too much and too little But my experience as an investor convinces me that the happyme rarelyseen. highs and lows as it does at the happymedium. Most investors’ attitu toward risk do the very same. What’s the greatest source of investment risk? Does it come fro economic developments? What Happens When RiskAversion Is Insufficien The Global Financial Crisisof 2007–08 represented the greatest finan downswing of my lifetime, and consequently it presents the best to observe, reflect and learn. Do you remember the game Bid-a-Note from the TV show Name That Tune?Contestant x said, “I can name that tune in six notes.” Then contestant y said, “I can name that tune in five notes.” T contestant x said, “I can name that tune in four notes.” The c who eventually got the chance to guess the name of the tune w one who was willing to accept the riskiest proposition—to tr basis of the least information.

What Happens When RiskAversion Is Excessive The symmetry of financial cycles, described at length in chap guaranteed that the risk-tolerantenvironment of 2005–07—and the boom financial instrument issuance that it abetted—would be followed by correction. And, of course, such a correction materialized. As I said earlier, the Global Financial Crisisof 2007–08 pro hope will turn out to be a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to witness irrational swingof the pendulum of emotion to total negativity, and the cycle in attitudes toward risk in the direction of excessive The actions that had been abetted by excessive risk tolerance in as just described—were exposed as having been foolhardy, and as they brought great pain and loss: It became clear that the effort to expand home ownership had purchases of homes by many people who couldn’t affordthem. Thousands lost the money they had put into home equity, along moving costs and any improvements they made. Sub-prime mortgages that had been issued without proof of incom employment were shown to have been unwise. Weakmortgage lending practices—along with extensive mortgage fraud that those practices failed to catch—turned out, not surprisingly, led to the issuance of many mortgages on whichborrowers were to make the required payments. The extrapolation of low historic default rates on mortgages have ignored the possibility that weakened mortgage lending practic would result in defaults at unprecedented rates. Since actualdefaults exceeded the historic rates that had been the for security structuring decisions, debt ratings and loss projections, structured and levered securities that had been built from sub-prime mortgages also defaulted in stunning numbers, proving their high to have been erroneous. Levered mortgage backed securities (and financial derivatives, most whichcontain high levelsof built-in leverage) generally switched being return-enhancing tools to weapons of financial mass destruction, as levered funds and securities breached loan covenants, and issuer ultimately proved unable to service their debt. Of course, the new financial products demonstrated—as usual—that financial innovations promising high returns with low risk rarelykeep that promise.

As for the relaxed regulations, the financial conglomerates permit revocation of the Glass-Steagall Act had extensive problems; the of the uptick rule allowed the stocks of financial institu down relentlessly;and several banksproved unable to survive high levelsof leverage that had been allowed. Because of everything that had gone before, the consequences inc massive mortgage defaults and home repossessions; downgradings and failures on the part of mortgage backed securities; collapsing home the inability to sell existing homes; collapsing stock and corporate markets and the disappearance of liquidity; a total drying up availability;and failures, bailouts and bankruptcies at a number Of course, it’s improbable events that brought on the credit c Lots of bad thingshappened that had been considered unlikely (if n impossible),and they happened at the same time, to investors who’d taken on significant leverage. (“TheLimits to Negativism,” Octobe 2008) What was the cumulative psychological effect of all of the aboveon investors and other financial-system participants? In short, it scared death.Whentotal fear replaces a high degree of confidence, excessive aversion takes the place of unrealistic risk tolerance. And that’s what happened in late 2008 following the bankruptcy of Lehman Brothers. came forward in droves, exhibiting a high degree of urgency. the sidelines. Asset pricescollapsed. And market liquidity went to zero. All these thingsresulted from the replacementof high risk tolerance wi high risk aversion. And they all contributed to still more fear, still more aversion, further negative events, and widespread fear of more of The same people who had bought untried instruments on the basis assumptions and promises that were too good to be true now were that the entire financial system mightmelt down. things. The cycles in psychology amply demonstratethat investors little of their time at the happymedium. The fluctuation—or inconstancy—in attitudes toward risk is both the of some cycles and the cause or exacerbator of others. And it on, since it seemsto be hard-wired into most people’s psyches to more optimistic and risk-tolerantwhen thingsare going well, and then more worried and risk-averse when thingsturn downward. That means they’re willing to buy when they should be most cautious, and most reluc when they should be most aggressive. Superior investors recognize this strive to behave as contrarians.

IX THE CREDIT CYCLE Superior investing doesn’t come from buying high-qualityassets, b from buying when the deal is good, the price is low, the potenti returnis substantial, and the risk is limited. Theseconditions are much more the case when the credit markets are in the less-euphoric more-stringent part of their cycle.The slammed-shut phase of the credit cycle probably does more to make bargains available than any other singlefactor. Now our foundation is complete. We’ve covered the economic cycles that provide the fundamental backdrop for investment activ also the psychological and attitudinal swings that occur in response changes in fundamentals (and that tend to exaggerate them). Now to take up some specific types of financial cycles. You will note fluctuations in all the abovestrongly affect the cycles covered in following chapters. As we’vealready discussed, some activities—like home buying—are highly responsive to movements in the economic cycle,and others—lik purchasing food—are not. Some cycles have a profound effect on other aspects of the economy and on other cycles, and some do not. this chapter, the credit cycle,is in each case the former: it is responsive to economic developments and highly influential. Lastly, extremely volatile. Thus its movements are powerful and extreme, and greatly affect activity in many other areas. And all these thingsare exacerbated by the swings of psychology described in chapters VII Please note that the subject here is sometimes referred to as market cycle ratherthan the credit cycle.I don’t find the distinction important. Strictly speaking, “capital” refers to all the money used a business, while “credit” refers to that portion of a company’s made up of debt ratherthan equity. In practice, these two cycle name be used interchangeably, although I do find many fewer references to capital market cycle.I’m going to stick to “the credit cycle” when

talking about debt markets, and I may lapse into “the capital mark when I’m talking about the general availability of financing. But eithe and most importantly,the considerations that apply to one apply equally the other. The following passage from my memo “Open and Shut” (Decemb calls on some of what I wrote earlier and places the credit cycle within the range of cycles: Consider this: the ups and downs of economies are usually b fluctuations in corporate profits, and fluctuations in profits for th and fall of securities markets. However, in recessions and recover economic growth usually deviates from its trendline rate by o few percentage points. Why, then, do corporate profits increase a decrease so much more? The answer lies in thingslike financi leverage and operating leverage, whichmagnify the impact on p of rising and falling revenues. And if profits fluctuate this way—more than GDP, but still relatively moderately—why is it that the securities markets soar a collapse so dramatically? I attribute this to fluctuations in p and, in particular, to the profound influence of psychology on th availability of capital. In short, whereas economies fluctuate a little and profits a the credit window openswide and then slams shut . . . thus th this memo. I believe the credit cycle is the most volatile o and has the greatest impact. Thus it deserves a great deal o And here’show I put it, more succinctly, in “You Can’t Predict. Yo Prepare.” (November 2001): The longer I’m involved in investing, the more impressed I a power of the credit cycle.It takes only a small fluctuation in th economy to produce a large fluctuation in the availability of c with great impact on asset pricesand back on the economy itself. Changes in the availability of capital or credit constitute one of fundamental influences on economies, companies and markets. Even thoug the credit cycle is less well-knownto the man on the street than most other cycles discussed in this book, I consider it to be of importance and profound influence. As suggested in the citation just above, the credit cycle can be understood through the metaphor of a window. In short, sometimes and sometimes it’s closed. And, in fact, people in the financial

frequent reference to just that: “the credit window,” as in “the place borrow money.” Whenthe window is open, financing is plentiful obtained, and when it’s closed, financing is scarce and hard to it’s essential to always bear in mind that the window can go to slammed shut in just an instant. There’s a lot more to this cycle—including the reasons for these cyclical movements and their impact—but that’s the bottom line. ∾ Why does this cycle have the importance I ascribe to it? First, capit is an essential ingredient in the productive process. Thus the abilit companies (and economies) to grow usually depends on the availability incremental capital. If the capital markets are closed, it can growth. Second, capital must be available in order for maturing debt to refinanced. Companies (as well as most other economic units, such as governments and consumers) generally don’t pay off their debts.Most of time they merely roll them over. But if a company is unable at the time that its existing debt comes due, it may default bankruptcy. Where we stand in the credit cycle—whether credit is readi available or difficult to obtain—is the greatest determinantof whet can be refinanced at a given time. Manycorporate assetsare long-term in nature (like buildings, machine vehicles and goodwill). Yet corporations often raise the money with buy those thingsby issuing short-term debt. They do this because the borrowing is generally lowest on short maturities. This arrangement —“borrowing short to investlong”—works out well most of the time, the credit market is open and fully functioning,meaning debt can be over with ease when it comes due. But the mismatch between longassetsthat can’t be easilyliquidated and shorter-term liabilities can easily bring on a crisis if the credit cycle turns negative so that maturing be refinanced. This classic mismatch, when combined with stringency in financing markets, is often the cause of the most spectacular financial meltdowns. Whenthe Global Financial Crisisbeganto build in 2007 and the cre markets froze up, the U.S. Department of the Treasury took the unprecedented step of guaranteeing all commercial paper. If it these debt instruments—which have maturities of 270 days or less—migh not have been capable of being rolledover, and thus could have caused defaults among even the strongest of companies. In fact, the defau well have been concentrated among top companies, since they issue commercial paper in billions precisely for the reason that their stron

creditworthiness gives them easy access to the commercial paper market. (The importance of the market being open—and of the ability to maturing paper—underlines the essential difference between positive worthand liquidity. Even a wealthy company can get into trouble have cash on hand and can’t obtain enough to meet its debt matu and other calls on cash as they arise.) Third, financial institutions represent a special, exaggeratedcase of on the credit markets. Financial institutions are in the business of money, and they need access to financing to keep that business also are often the site of the greatest short/long mismatches and poten meltdowns. Consider, for example, the bank that takes deposits that can withdrawn any day and uses them to make mortgage loans that won’tbe repaid for thirty years.What happens if all the depositors demand back on the same bad day (a “run on the bank”)? If there’s credit market (and no governmentbailout), that bank may fail. Fourth and finally, the credit market gives off signals that ha psychological impact. A closed credit market causes fear to of proportion to businesses’ negative realities. Difficult conditions can the capital market to close . . . and closed capital impact on business conditions (as well as on market participants businesses).This kind of “vicious circle” is part of most financial The Credit Cycle in Operation By now you should have an understanding of the nature and credit cycle.The next topic to tackleis why credit cycles occur: what credit to be more available at some times and less available at other The credit window doesn’t have a mind of its own, opening an its own accord. Rather, it follows events elsewhere. In “You You Can Prepare.” (November 2001), I put the expand-and-contract in context and explained it in some detail: The process is simple: The economy moves into a period of prosperity. Providers of capital thrive, increasing their capital base. Because bad news is scarce, the risks entailed in lending investing seem to have shrunk. Risk averseness disappears.

Financial institutions move to expand their businesses—that is, to provide more capital. They compete for market share by lowering demanded returns (e.g., cutting interest rates), lowering credit standards, providin more capital for a given transaction, and easing covenants. At the extreme, providers of capital finance borrowers and p that aren’tworthy of being financed. As The Economist said earlier this year, “the worst loans are made at the best of times.” This le capital destruction—that is, to the investment of capital in p wherethe cost of capital exceeds the returnon capital, and e to cases wherethere is no returnof capital. Whenthis point is reached, the up-leg is reversed. Losses cause lenders to become discouragedand shy away. Risk averseness rises, and along with it, interest rates, credit restrictions and covenant requirements. Less capital is made available—and at the trough of th only to the most qualified of borrowers. Companies become starved for capital. Borrowers are unable roll over their debts,leading to defaults and bankruptcies. This process contributes to and reinforces the economic contraction. Of course, at the extreme the process is ready to be r Because the competitionto make loans or investmentsis low, high returns can be demanded along with high creditworthiness. Contrarians who commit capital at this point have a shot at h returns, and those tempting potential returns begin to draw in c In this way, a recovery begins to be fueled. Sometimes people are eager to put money to work, and that ma credit window open wide. But when circumstances cause them to change minds, financing can become unavailable.As with so many other thing this book, it’s essential that the reader gain a clear understanding workings of the cycles, and especially of the way in whicheach to the next. Thus the thorough, step-by-stepexplanation provided here is essential and must be absorbed. But in “You Can’t Predict. You Can Prepare.” I went on to red process to just a few words. They truly do constitute the botto credit cycle,and they make clear the never-ending chain-reaction nature the cycle:

Prosperity brings expanded lending, whichleads to unwise lending whichproduces large losses, whichmakes lenders stop lending, w ends prosperity, and on and on. A market is an auction housewherethe item being offered go person who’ll pay the most for it. The financial markets are opportunity to make an investment or provide a loan goes to the participant who’ll pay the most for that opportunity.The bidding takes price to higher dollarlevelsand higher valuation parameters (such as price/earnings ratios). In the credit market, a high price or high parameter translates directly into a low yield on the debt instrument question, and the chance to provide capital goes to the lende the lowest yield. My memo “The Race to the Bottom,” published in February 20 primarily about the eagerness of providers of capital to expand business” in good times,and its effect: It helps to think of money as a commodity.Everyone’s money prettymuch the same. Yet institutions seeking to add to loan volume and private equity funds and hedgefunds seeking to increase th fees, all want to move more of it. So if you want to place m —that is, get people to go to you instead of your c financing—you have to make your money cheaper. As with the o commodities, low price is the most dependable route to increased market share. One way to lower the price for your money is by reducing interest rate you charge on loans.A slightly more subtleway is agree to a higher price for the thing you’re buying, such a a higher p/e ratio for a common stock or a higher to price when you’re buying a company. Any way you slice it, y settling for a lower prospective return. But there are other ways to cheapen your money, and they’re the primary subject of th With debt, structure is important. Thus easierterms for the borrower ca bring addedrisk for the lender. For example, lenders would like protective covenants that limit the extentto whicha borrower can enga certain actions that increase the risk that is present. They may debt the borrower can assume, limit the cash it can pay out in require that it maintain a certain minimum net worth. But, credit market is heated, the “best buyer” of debt—the most eager

mightbe willing to accept a structure with fewer covenants, and risk. So in an auction, the chance to make a loan or buy a the provider of capital who’swilling to accept a combinatio and the riskiest structure. Whenrisk aversion is present and the lending opportunities is reasonable relative to the supply of opportunities, the bidding is usually prudent. But when risk tolera over and lenders compete avidly for opportunities, the bidding become overheated. The resulting opportunity to lend is likely to be high a price: a yield that’s too low and/or risk that’s excessive. Thus overheated auction in the credit market—as elsewhere—is likely to produ “winner” who’sreally a loser. This is the process I call the race On the other hand, there are times when buyers show up for auctions small numbers, and the few who do attendare interested in buying giveaway prices. The bidding stalls, and the result is low prices, yields, and loan structures that affordexcellent protection. Unlike the overheated climate that spawns the race to the bottom, ice-cold whichno one’s eager to lend can createreal winners. The degree of openness of the credit window depends almost whether providers of capital are eager or reticent, and it has impact on economies, companies, investors, and the prospective returnand riskiness of the investment opportunities that result. In the short term, the effect of generous capital market conditi make more money available to more companies for more reasons, a lower rates of interest and with fewer covenants. This leads to higher levelsof acquisitions, buyouts and corporate expansion (not to mention rapid recapitalizations of buyout companies and thus high short-term rates of return). In the short run, this contributes to a level of general financial activity. Another effect is to forestall financial stringency at weak companies. Whenlenders are strict and covenants are tight, operating problems can lead quickly to both technical defaults (violations o covenants) and “money defaults” (non-payment of interest or principal). But looserconditions can permit default to be forestal if covenants are lax or absent; if borrowers have the option cash-pay bondsinto payment-in-kind bonds(through a recent innovation, “toggle bonds”); or if they can raise new money a postpone the day of reckoning. Eventually, many of the forestalled defaults will demonstratetheir inevitability, with the companies falling from more highly leverag heights. And certainly the capital markets’ willingness to finance

than-deserving companies will lead ultimately to a higher level o corporate distress. Thus, everything else being equal,the bigger the boom—and the greater the excesses of the capital markets in upward direction—the greater the bust. Timing and extentare n predictable, but the occurrence of cycles is the closest thing I inevitable. (“TheRace to the Bottom,” February 2007) The Impact of the Credit Cycle One of the main pointsin this book is the extentto whichevents cycle have influence in other fields and on other kinds of cycles. Now this clearer than in the credit cycle. In “Genius Isn’t Enough” on the subject of Long-Term Capital Management (October 1998), I wrote “Look around the n there’s a crisis;you’llprobably find a lender.” Over-permissive providers of capital frequently aid and abet financial bubbles. In each case, lenders and investors provided too much cheapmoney and the r was over-expansion and dramatic losses. In the movie Fields Dreams KevinCostner was told, “If you build it, they will come.” the financial world, if you offer cheapmoney, they will borrow and build—often without discipline, and with very negative consequences. The capital cycle contributed tremendously to the tech bubble. This ease c more optical fiber capacity to be built than was needed at th lot of whichis sitting idle. Muchof the investment that went in

may never be recovered. Once again,easy money has led to capital destruction. In making investments, it has become my habit to worr the economic future—which I’m sure I can’t know much about—than I do about the supply/demand picture relating to capital. The wide-open capital market arises when the news is good, asset pricesare rising, optimis is ridinghigh, and all thingsseem possible. X THE DISTRESSED DEBT CYCLE Few lenders and bond buyers are imprudent enough to advanc money that won’tbe repaid if conditions remain as they a sober times,they insist on a margin of safetysufficient to ensure interest and principal will be paid even if conditions for the d deteriorate. This process, as w Oaktree, “stacks the logs in the fireplace” for the next bonfire I was fortunate to partner with BruceKarshthree decades ago, an in 1988 we formed our first fund for distressed debt investing, whic believe was one of the very first from a mainstream financial instit took us into a highly specialized investment niche. Rather than companies that are doing well or have brightfutures, ou distressed debt investmentsare generally in companies that are doing so poorly that they’ve defaulted on their outstanding debt or are consi highly likely to do so: they’re either in bankruptcy or viewed To be clear, our typical company isn’t challenged operationally, just overloaded with debt; thus our mantra is “goodcompany, bad balance Normally investors buy debt securities or make loans because they ex to be paid interest periodicallyand have their principal repaid when matures. With distressed debt, however, the consensus is that these thing won’toccur: instead it is expected that the debt won’t“stay curre serviced.” So if interest and principal aren’texpected to be paid, distressed debt investor’s motivation? The answer is that debtholders who aren’tpaid as scheduled have a “creditor claim” against the debtor. In short—and to over-simpl company goes through bankruptcy, the old owners are wipedout and

creditors become the new owners. Each creditor receives his share value of the company—depending on the amount and seniority of holds—in some combination of cash, new debt and ownership of the company going forward. A distressed debt investor tries to figureout (a) what the bankrupt is worth(or will be worthat the time it emerges from bankruptcy that value will be divided among the company’s creditors and other and (c) how long this process will take. With correct answers to questions, he can determine what the annual returnwill be on a company’s debt if purchased at a given price. Getting our start in distressed debt investing in 1988 was extremel advantageous, as there were few competitors and the field was little know and little understood—two conditions that can help make for superior retur possible in any field. As a result, our funds have been able to average returnover the 29 years since. But, as with many things, the is of only limited significance. The funds we formed at less-t generally have yielded good returns, but the results on funds form right times have been superlative. In other words, the opportunities for top returns in distresse go. The first of the two essential ingredients in their creation consists unwise extension of credit.” Giventhe discussion in the last chap should have an understanding of what I have in mind and how I’ll explain through the example of high yield bonds:

At the start, appropriately risk-averse investors apply stringent credit standards to the issuance of high yield bonds. The same healthy economic environment that facilitates bond issuan makes it easy for companies to service their existing debt defaults are scarce). Thus high yield bonds—with their generous interest coupons and damage from defaults—provide solid realized returns. Thosereturns convince investors that high yield bond investing is attracting increased capital to the market. Increased capital for investment translates into increased demand bonds. Since Wall Streetnever allows demand to go unmet, in increased bond issuance. The same condition that allows largeramounts of bondsto be strong investor demand—invariably also allows bondsof lesser creditworthiness to be issued. Few lenders and bond buyers are imprudent enough to advance won’tbe repaid if conditions remain as they are. And in sober insist on a margin of safetysufficient to ensure, as I said and principal will be paid even if conditions for the debtor deter But when the credit market heats up—when the race to the bottom avid lenders to finance less-deserving borrowers and accept weak structures—bondsare issued that lack that margin of safetyand won’ to be serviced if thingsget a little worse. This is the unwi credit. This process, as noted earlier, “stacks the logs in the next bonfire. But that’s only the first half the process. Even after the fuel for a has been assembled, there won’tbe a conflagration until the second arrives: an igniter. It usually comes in the form of a corporate profits to decline. This is often accompanied by a the slamming shut of the credit window—such that existing debt can’t refinanced and goes into default instead. And often conditions are exace by exogenous events that sap confidence and damage the economy financial markets. In 1990 these consisted of: the Gulf War, whichwas set off by the Iraqi invasion of Kuwa the bankruptcy of many of the prominent, highly levered buyou 1980s; and the imprisonment of Michael Milken (the principal investment behind high yield bonds) and the collapse of Drexel Burnh employer and the investment bank most closely associated with high yield bonds). With Drexel and Milken gone from the scene,

exchanges that had helped weakened companies avoid default were to effect. Whenthe igniter arrives, bondsthat shouldn’t have been issued—and perhaps even some that should have—begin to fail. A slower economy makes it harder for companies to With the credit market closed, refinancing can’t be accomplished, meaning defaults rise. Rising defaults damage investor psychology. Investors who were risk-tolerantwhen thingswere going well now become risk-averse. Advancing capital to financially distressed companies—which seeme like a good idea just a short time earlier—now goes out of Potential debt buyers back away,refusing to “catch a falling saying they’ll wait until the uncertainty has been resolved. Capital that is mobile flees from the market. Buyers becom sellers predominate. Selling of bondsincreases; bond pricescascade downward; funds that receive withdrawalsbecome forced sellers; and eventually bonds available for sale at any price. Theseare the conditions that give rise to the ability to effect ba purchases of distressed debt, and thus to opportunities for elevated Of course, the cycle doesn’t go in just one direction. Eventual economy begins to recover and the credit market reopens. Thes developments cause the default rate on high yield bondsto recede. combination of an improving economy and a declining default selling to abate.Thus the downward pressure on bond priceseases,and buying begins. Pricesrise ratherthan fall, and balance sheet restructurin restore companies to viability, remove stumbling blocks and unloc Whengains on bond purchases made at the bottom begin to be notic additional capital is attracted to the market. The combination and increased capital causes the demand for bondsto grow. And we’vegone full cycle:we’re back to the beginning. A while back I put together a concise synopsis of the wa issuance underlays the rise and fall of the distressed debt cycle.Here Risk-averse investors limit quantities issued and demand high quality High-quality issuance leads to low default rates. Low default rates cause investors to become complacent and risktolerant.

Risk tolerance opensinvestors to increased issuance and lower quality Lower-quality issuance eventually is testedby economic difficulty gives rise to increased defaults. Increased defaults have a chilling effect, making investors once more. And so it resumes. This is a cycle I’ve seen in actionrepeatedly. My 29 years of ex distressed debt tells me its themes definitely do rhyme. And the description of the cycle gives me an excellent opportunity to make my that each event in a cycle causes the one that follows. In fact, description specifically to achieve that goal. This is a true chain reaction, expect to continue in the future. ∾ As you can see, the rise and fall of opportunities in the mark debt stems from the interaction of other cycles: in the economy, inves psychology,risk attitudes and the credit market. The economic cycle influences investor psychology,company profitabilityand the incidence of default. The cycle in attitudes toward risk facilitates the issuance of at the top and denies capital for refinancing at the bottom The credit cycle has a profound effect on the availability of refina and the degree to whichwould-be debt issuers are subjected credit standards. Hopefully it’s clear that multiple underlying cycles have effects on distressed debt market that are far from discrete and isolated. XI THE REAL ESTATE CYCLE Muchof investing is subject to gross generalizations and sweepin statements—usually stressing the positives, because of humans’ tendency toward greed and wishful thinking—and for some reason this seemsparticularly true in real estate. Over the course of m careerI’ve heard investment in real estate rationalized by easily digested statements like “they’re not making any more” (in connection with land), “you can always live in it” (in connection w houses), and “it’s a hedgeagainst inflation” (in connection with properties of all types). What people eventually learn is that regardless of the merit behind these statements, they won’tprotect a investment that was made at too high a price. The cycle in real estate has a lot in common with other cycles one that controls the provision of capital or credit. Positive events and increased profitabilitylead to greater enthus optimism. Improved psychology encourages increased activity. That includes doing more of something; doing it on the basis of rosier assumptions; higher pricesto do it; and/or lowering the standards that if one is to do it. All of these thingstend to entail the greater risk. The combination of positive psychology and the increase in causes asset pricesto rise, whichencourages still more activity, furthe price increases, and greater risk-bearing. Inevitably this virtuous circle takes on the appearance of being unstoppable, and this appearance causes asset pricesand the level activity to go too far to be sustained. But when the news eventually turns less positive and the environment becomes less hospitable, the levelsof psychology,activity and risk-bearin prove to have been excessive, and the same goes for asset prices. The

resulting price correction causes psychology to become less positive, causes disinvestment, whichputs further downward pressure on price forth. Theseare all elements that most financial cycles have in common includes the cycle in real estate. But the real estate cycle incorporate ingredient that the othersgenerally don’t share:the long lead times required for real estate development to take place. In the credit market, for instance, good news and rosy psychology wi to increased lending as soon as investment bankers can line up borrowers and print prospectuses. Thus lenders’ increased ardor translates almost instantaneously into increased demand for securities, lower dema yields, lower lending standards, and increased levelsof lending and issuance. But in the market for physical real estate—the worldof so-called and mortar”—there can be significant delays. Before a new build come to market, adding to the supply of space (and thus puttin pressure on pricesfor space if demand does not increase apace), feasibility studies have to be performed; a site has be foundand the building has to be designed; environmental impact studies performed; permission to build has to be secured from authorities, and sometimes zoning modifications; financing has to be obtained; and construction has to be completed. This process can run to sever the case of a majorproject it can exceed a decade. But change very significantly in the interim. I’ll use a description of the cycle in real estate development fro (January 2013)to illustrate. As I said there, “it’s usually clear, simp regularly recurring”: Bad times cause both the level of building activity to be low the availability of capital for building to be constrained. In a while the times become less bad, and eventually even go Bettereconomic times cause the demand for premises to rise. With few buildings having been started during the soft pe and now coming on stream, this additional demand for sp causes the supply/demand picture to tighten and thus ren sale pricesto rise. This improves the economics of real estate ownership, reawakening developers’ eagerness to build. The better times and improved economics also make providers of capital more optimistic. Their improved state of mind causes financing to become more readily available.

Cheaper, easierfinancing raises the pro formareturns on potential projects, adding to their attractiveness and increasin developers’ desireto pursue them. Higher projected returns, more-optimistic developers and moregenerous providers of capital combine for a ramp-up in starts. The first completed projects encounter strong pent-up demand. They lease up or sell out quickly, giving their developers go returns. Thosegood returns—plus each day’s increasinglypositive headlines—cause still more buildings to be planned, financed and green-lighted. Cranes fill the sky (and additional cranes are ordered fro factory, but that’s a different cycle). It takes years for the buildings started later to reach completi In the interim, the first ones to open eat into the unmet The period between the start of planning and the opening building is often long enough for the economy to transitio boom to bust. Projects started in good times often open in ba times,meaning their space adds to vacancies, putting downward pressure on rents and sale prices. Unfilled space hangsover th market. Bad times cause the level of building activity to be low an availability of capital for building to be constrained. Note that as in the case of many of the other cycles discusse step leads to the next. In particular, the step at the bottom of the one that kicks off the next iteration. This is a good example whichcycles are self-perpetuating. ∾ In lending, since the time lags inherent in the process are brief and business conditions in force at the time that the will to lend loan is conceived generally are still in force when the loan is funde conditions change materially in the relatively brief interim, the lende able to pull his commitment under a “material adverse change” the contract. Thus there’s relatively little risk in general lending from the gap between idea and action. But given the many years that can pass between a building’s concepti and its opening, conditions can change enormously, as just descr adds an element to real estate development that makes it poten Developers hope this risk will be offset by the fact that they can utiliz

extensive external financing (and thus they risk relatively little of their money and are able to lever up the returnon it to a great WhenI moved to Los Angeles in 1980, steel skeletons stood alo “Wilshire corridor” in Westwood, wheregleaming condominium buildings had been imagined. The developers who had initiated these projects in 1970sboom were caught out.
