XII PUTTING IT ALL TOGETHER—THE MARKET CYCLE The first time an inexperienced investor lives through an upward market cycle,the beginnings of the progression may seem logical, a the positives compound in a bull market or bubble. The f much good news and good feeling can end in lossescan come as a surprise. It is inescapable that it will seem so to the uninitia course, because if the progressions weren’t permitted to g extremes on the basis of errorsin judgment, markets wouldn’t r bull market tops to collapse from (or bear market bottoms to from). Our job as investors is simple: to deal with the pricesof wherethey stand today and making judgments regarding how they will change in the future. Pricesare affected primarily by developme areas:fundamentals and psychology. Fundamentals, whichI’ve been calling “events,” can largely be to earnings, cash flow, and the outlook for the two. They are many things, including trendsin the economy, profitabilityand the availability of capital. And psychology—howinvestors feel about fundamentals and value them—is likewise affected by many things, particularly investors’ of optimism and attitude toward risk. Thereare cycles in the elements mentioned above, and several each cycle.The themes behind the cycles’ behavior—and the ways they interact and combine—have a repeating, understandable pattern, have discussed at length. They all come together—and they combine idiosyncratic and random influences—to cause the behavior of the market.

It’s my goal in this chapter to give you a feel for the cy downs of the market. Not for the fact that it rises and falls, so in the past, or what these movements were in reaction to. But forces—andspecifically the non-fundamental, non-economic forces—that cause it to go up and down, often in manicfashion. If the market were a disciplined calculator of value based exclusiv company fundamentals, the price of a security wouldn’t fluctuate than the issuer’s current earnings and the outlook for earnings fact, the price generally should fluctuate less than earnings, since quart quarter changes in earnings often even out in the long run and, necessarily reflect actualchanges in the company’s long-term potential. And yet security pricesgenerally fluctuate much more than earnings. Th reasons, of course, are largely psychological, emotional and non-f Thus price changes exaggerate and overstate fundamental changes. Here shorthand version as to why: Events in the economy and in corporate profits turn increa positive. Positive events feed investor psychology.Emotion, so-called “anim spirits” and investors’ tolerance for risk all rise with those positiv events (or sometimes despite negative ones). Rising psychology causes investors to be less demanding in protection and prospective return. The combination of positive events, strengthening psychology lowering of investors’ returnrequirements causes asset pricesto Eventually, however, the process goes into reverse. Events fail to expectations, perhaps because the environment that produ becomes less hospitable, or perhaps because expectations were unrealistically high. Investors eventually prove that psychology can’t remain positive foreve Cooler heads conclude that priceshave reached levelsthat are unjustified, or psychology may softenfor any of a million possib reasons (or for no apparent reason). Pricesfall when events are less positive or come to be viewe positively. Sometimes it happens simply because priceshave reache levelsthat are unstainable,or because of negative developments environment. Having turned downward, asset pricescontinue to decline until so low that the stage is set for their recovery. It’s important to understand the way fundamentals and psychology int as described above. But it’s essential that I repeatsomething abou

process: while the description aboveis of one that is orderly and the process is nowhere as neat as this description may make it sequence in whichthese thingsoccur is subject to change, as is direction of causality. Sometimes events cause psychology to strengthen, and sometimes improving psychology has a positive impact on events (for bolstering the economy and corporate profits). And while it’s obvious that improved investor psychology makes pricesrise, it’s just as obvious that rising pricesmake investors feel wealthier, smarter and more optimistic. So, in other words, these relationships can work in both direction even do so simultaneously. And each can cause the other. The speed at thingsplay out is highly variable from cycle to cycle and over the given cycle.And lastly,cycles don’t necessarily progress smoothly; rather, they can be marked by dips, recoveries and feints along the way. It’s for reasons like these that investing can’t be described as sci can’t be depended on to work the same every time. I keep coming Mark Twain’s observation that “history doesn’t repeatitself, but it does rhyme.” The reasons and results are never the same as in the usually reminiscent of developments we’veseen before. Regardless of the imprecision of the process, it’s clear that past ev expected futureevents combine with psychology to determine asset price Events and psychology also influence the availability of credit, and availability of credit greatly affects asset prices, just as it feeds influence events and psychology. In sum, these thingsall come together to createthe market cycle.W about it every day, most prominentlyin connection with the ups and down the stock market, but also with regard to markets for thingslike and currencies. This is wheremany cycles intersect, and it’s the subje chapter. ∾ Financial theory portrays investors as “economic men”:objective, ration optimizers. Thus it suggests that the market they collectively form is author and investor (and Warren Buffett’s teacher) Ben Graham “weighing machine”: a disciplined assessor of the value of assets. In stark contrast, however, the truth is that financial facts and fig only a starting point for market behavior; investor rationality is not the rule; and the market spends little of its time calmly data and setting pricesfree of emotionality.

Investment fundamentals are ratherstraightforward. Past events have already taken place and been recorded, and many people have the quan skills required to analyze them. Current performance is captured statements, whichsometimes present an accurate picture and sometimes require skillful adjustment. And futureevents are unknown to every (although some investors are better able than othersto foresee them). Fundamentals aren’tthe most variable part of investing or the part intrigues me most. And anyway, I can’t write a book telling you more than othersabout futureevents. Doing a superior job of that elements of foresight, intuition and “second-level thinking” that I be reduced to paper or taught. The part of investing that fascinates me—that I find myself thi most, and wheremy Oaktree colleagues and I have made some of our contributions to our clients’ welfare—regards the ways in whichinves deviate from the assumption of rationality, and the contributionof those to the oscillation of cycles. A large number of elements play parts in this aspect of inv making, interfering with the process of arriving at purely econ They may come under the headings of human nature, psychology or —the distinction isn’t straightforward or important for our purposes here— and they absolutely are capable of dominating investor behavior and markets. Some but not all vary cyclically, and they all can affect or cycles. Here are the most important influences: the way investors fluctuate ratherthan hold firmly to rational and the resulting rational decisions; the tendency of investors to hold distorted viewsof what’s engaging in selective perception and skewed interpretation; quirks like confirmation bias, whichmakes people accept confirms their thesis and reject that whichdoesn’t, and the tendency toward non-linear utility, whichcauses most people to value more highly than a dollarmade (or a dollarof potential profit the gullibility that makes investors swallow tall tales of profit good times,and the excessive skepticism that makes them reject all possibility of gains in bad times; the fluctuating nature of investors’ risk tolerance and risk aversi thus of their demands for compensatory risk premiums; the herd behavior that results from pressure to fall into line othersare doing, and as a result the difficulty of holding positions; the extreme discomfort that comes from watching othersmake money doing something you’ve rejected;

thus the tendency of investors who have resisted an asset bubble ultimately succumb to the pressure, throwin the towel and buy though—no, because—the asset that is the subject of the appreciated substantially); the corresponding tendency to give up on investmentsthat are and unsuccessful, no matter how intellectually sound, and finally, the fact that investing is all about money, whichintrod powerful elements such as greed for more, envy of the money making, and fear of loss. Bulls and Bears Investors have been characterized for at least a hundred years as (optimists who think stocks will rise and behave aggressively as “bears” (pessimists who think they’ll fall and who thus behave defen Consequently, people apply the label of “bull market” to a mark risen, is rising or will rise (it’s quite imprecise) and “bear market” to opposite. About 45 years ago—in the early 1970s—I received one of the gifts I was ever given, when an older and wiser investor introduced three stagesof a bull market”: the first stage, when only a few unusually perceptive people believ thingswill get better, the second stage, when most investors realize that improvement actually taking place,and the third stage, when everyone concludes thingswill get better forever. The arrival of this simple truth opened my eyes to the no psychological extremes and the impact of those extremes on mark Like many of the great quotations and adages, it captures disproporti wisdom in a few simple words. It’s all about the changeabil pattern they follow over the course of a cycle,and how they error. In the first stage, because the possibility of improvement is inv most investors and thus unappreciated, security pricesincorporate little or optimism. Often the first stage occurs after priceshave been pounded in crash,and the same downtrend that decimated pricesalso has wipedout psychology,turning the members of the crowd against the mark them to swearoff investing forever.

In the last stage, on the other hand, events have gone well for so have been reflected so powerfully in asset prices, further liftingthe the market—that investors extrapolate improvement to infinity and pricesto reflect their optimism. Trees generally don’t grow to the sky, this stage investors act as if they will . . . and pay up they perceive. Few thingsare as costlyas paying for potential that have been overrated. It follows from the abovethat someone who invests in the fir when almost no one can see a reason for optimism—buys asset pricesfrom whichsubstantial appreciation is possible. But someone who in the third stage invariably pays a high price for the market’s exces enthusiasm and loses money as a result. The description of the three stagesof the bull market offersa lot with great economy. But not long after learning about the three stages, across something even better and briefer—essentially the same message just 14 words: “What the wise man does in the beginning, the end.” I consider this the number-onepiece of investment wisdom and an incredible distillation of the import of cycles. Again, the early who by definition has to be that rare person who sees the futur othersand has the inner strength to buy without validation from the garners undiscovered potential at a bargain price. But every inves eventually is overdone and bid up too far, so that the buyer in for potential that is overrated. He ends up with capital punishmen appreciation. “What the wise man does in the beginning, the fool does in the you 80% of what you have to know about market cycles and their Warren Buffett has said much the same thing even more concisely: “Firs innovator, then the imitator, then the idiot.” Of course, cycles work in both directions, and the depths of Financial Crisisgave me an opportunity to invertthe old saying and the three stagesof a bear market in “The Tide Goes Out” (March the first stage, when just a few thoughtful investors recognize that, despite the prevailing bullishness, thingswon’talways be rosy, the second stage, when most investors recognize that thingsare deteriorating, and the third stage, when everyone’s convinced thingscan only get worse. I’ve mentioned capitulation before. It’s a fascinating phenomenon, an there’s a dependable cycle to it, too. In the first stage of eithe market, most investors refrain (by definition) from joining in on

only a tiny minority does. This may be because they lack the speci that underlies that action; the ability to act before the case and othershave flocked to it (after whichit’s no longer unapprecia reflected in market prices); or the spine needed to take a the herd and behave as a non-conforming contrarian. Having missed the opportunity to be early, bold and right, investors continue to resist as the movement takes hold and gathers steam. has resulted in market movement, they still may not join in. With discipline, they refuseto buy into the market, asset class or industry that has been lifted by bullish buyers, or to sell once selling caused pricesto fall belowintrinsic value.It’s not for them to join late. But most investors do capitulate eventually. They simply run out of resolve needed to hold out. Once the asset has doubled or triple the way up—or halved on the way down—many people feel wrong, and are so envious of those who’ve profited from the stepped the decline, that they lose the will to resist further. My on this subject is from Charles Kindleberger: “There is nothi to one’s well-being and judgment as to see a friendget rich” (Man and Crashes: A History of Financial Crises, 1989). Market pained by the money that othershave made and they’ve missed they’re afraidthe trend (and the pain) will continue further. They conc that joining the herd will stop the pain, so they surrender. Eventually the asset well into its rise or sell after it has fallen a great deal. In other words, after failing to do the right thing in stage on compound the error by taking that actionin stage three, when it has the wrong thing to do. That’s capitulation. It’s a highly investor behavior during cycles, and a great example of psych error at its worst. Of course, when the last resister has given up and bought we —or sold well into the decline—there’s no one left to fall in line. buyers means the end of the bull market, and vice versa.The makes the top or bottom and sets the scene for a cyclical direction. He is the “fool in the end.” The following account from history shows that even the most bri among us can fall prey to capitulation: Sir Isaac Newton, who was the Master of the Mint at the ti “South Sea Bubble,” joined many other wealthy Englishmenin investing in the stock [of the SouthSea Company]. It rose from £ in January of 1720 to £1,050 in June. Early in this rise, h Newton realized the speculative nature of the boom and sold h

£7,000 worthof stock.Whenasked about the direction of the market he is reported to have replied “I can calculate the motion heavenly bodies, but not the madness of the people.” By September 1720, the bubble was punctured and the stock price fell below£200, off 80% from its high three months earlier. It tu out, however, that despite having seen through the bubble e Isaac, like so many investors over the years,couldn’t stand the pressure of seeing those around him make vast profits. He b back the stock at its high and endedup losing£20,000. Not even o of the world’s smartest men was immune to this tangible le gravity! (“bubble.com,” January 2000) Bubbles and Crashes We’ve always had rising and falling markets, and we always continue to a significant extent, they’re calledbull markets and Even further and they’re calledbooms, manias and crazes; busts panics. The most popular terms today for describing extreme bull and markets are “bubble” and “crash.” Theselatter terms have been around for a long time. The “South Se Bubble” cited above, a maniafor investing in the company that would pay off the national debt by exploiting a monopoly to America, caught England by storm in 1720. And the market colla kicked off the Great Depression is calledthe Great Crashof 1929. But the “tech bubble,” “Internet bubble” and “dot-com bubble” of 1995 and the housing and mortgage bubbles that endedin 2007, bringing significant crashes in markets around the world—thatbrought the “bubble” into everyday use. As a result of the foregoing, there’s a tendency these days—es the part of the media—to call any big market rise a bubble. in fall 2017, the S&P 500 index of U.S. stocks has roughly quad (including dividends) from its low in March 2009, and the yield on yield bondshas fallen to a piddling 5.8%.Thus I’m often asked whether we’re in a new bubble of one kind or another, perhaps imply imminent. That’s why I want to spendsome time on my conviction every big rise is a bubble. For me, the term “bubble” has speci psychological connotations that should be understood and looked I’ve lived through bubbles much older than those in tech stocks an housing mentioned on the preceding page. One of the best examples 1960smaniafor the “Nifty Fifty”stocks—the shares of the highest-qua

fastest-growing companies in America. As far as I’m concerned, there common thread that runs through bubbles and was exemplifiedby Fifty: conviction that, as far as the subject asset is concerned, “ther thing as a price too high.” And of course it follows that you pay, you’re sure to make money. There’s only one form of intelligent investing, and that’s figuring ou something’sworthand buying it for that price or less. You can’t have intelligent investing in the absence of quantification of value and on an attractive purchase price. Any investment movement that’s built aroun a concept other than the relationship between price and value is irrati The idea of “growth stocks” beganto be popularized in the ea based on the goal of participating in the rapidly growing profi benefitting from advances in technology, marketing and management techniques. It gathered steam, and by 1968, when I had a summ investment research department of First National City Bank (the predecesso of Citibank), the Nifty Fifty stocks—the fastest growing and best—had appreciated so much that the bank trust departmentsthat did most of the investing in those days generally lost interest in all other stocks. Everyone wanted a piece of Xerox, IBM, Kodak, Polaroid, M Hewlett-Packard, TexasInstruments, Coca-Cola and Avon. Thesecompanies were considered to be so great that nothing bad could ever happen And it was accepted dictum that it absolutely didn’tmatter what paid. If it was a little too high, no matter: the companies’ fast-r would soon grow into it. The result was predictable. Whenever people are willing to invest regardless of price, they’re obviously doing so based on emotion and popularity ratherthan cold-blooded analysis. So Nifty Fifty stocks that been selling at 80–90 times earnings in 1968, in the vanguard bull market, came down to earth when ardor cooled. Thus many sold at times earnings in the much weaker stock market of 1973, meaning in “America’s best companies” had lost 80–90% of their money. And that several of the “flawless” companies mentioned have since gone bank or experiencedserious brushes with distress. So much for “no price too high.” No asset or company is so can’t become overpriced. Certainly that notion must have been banished forever. But lest you think this lesson truly was learned, let’s fast-forward 1990s. Now it was technology stocks that were commanding wide attention. Just as corporate innovation had sparked the growth stock gains in telecommunications (cell phones and transmission via optic media(including the limitless demand for “content” to fill the new

entertainment channels), and information technology (especially the Internet) were firing investors’ imaginations. “The Internet will change the world,” was the battle cry, followed by “for an e-commerce stock,there’s no price too high.” Whe Fifty stocks had sold at inflated multiples of their companies’ earni wasn’t a problem with Internet stocks: these companies had no only was the investing purely conceptual, but so were many of the So instead of p/e ratios, the stocks sold at multiples of any) or “eyeballs”: the number of consumers visiting their websites. Just as with the Nifty Fifty, there was a grain of truth underlying the investment fad; one is usually required in order for a bubble investors slipped the moorings of reason and discipline when they that price didn’tmatter. Some saw clients withdraw a large part some became dispirited and quit the business, and othersgave up and into the bubble . . . just in time to see it collapse, ∾ The following progression serves to sum up regarding the upswing market cycle.It shows how cycles in economics, profits, psych aversion and mediabehavior combine to move market priceswell beyo intrinsic value,and how one development contributes to the next. The economy is growing, and the economic reports are positiv Corporate earnings are rising and beating expectations. The mediacarry only good news.

Securities markets strengthen. Investors grow increasinglyconfident and optimistic. Risk is perceived as being scarce and benign. Investors think of risk-bearingas a sure route to profit. Greedmotivates behavior. Demand for investment opportunities exceeds supply. Asset pricesrise beyond intrinsic value. Capital markets are wide open, making it easy to raise money over debt. Defaults are few. Skepticism is low and faith is high, meaning risky deals can be No one can imagine thingsgoing wrong. No favorable developmen seemsimprobable. Everyone assumes thingswill get better forever. Investors ignore the possibility of loss and worryonly about missin opportunities, No one can think of a reason to sell, and no one is Buyers outnumber sellers. Investors would be happyto buy if the market dips. Pricesreach new highs. Media celebrate this exciting event. Investors become euphoric and carefree. Security holders marvel at their own intelligence; perhaps more. Thosewho’ve remained on the sidelines feel remorse; thus they capitulate and buy. Prospective returns are low (or negative). Risk is high. Investors should forgetabout missing opportunity and worryonly about losingmoney. This is the time for caution! The most important thing to note is that maximum psychology,maximum availability of credit, maximum price, minimum potential returnand maximum risk all are reached at the same time, and usually these coincide with the last paroxysm of buying. Likewise, the following progression outlines what happens in a ma downswing. The economy is slowing; reports are negative. Corporate earnings are flat or declining, and falling short of Media reportonly bad news.

Securities markets weaken. Investors become worried and depressed. Risk is seen as being everywhere. Investors see risk-bearingas nothing but a way to lose money Fear dominates investor psychology. Demand for securities falls short of supply. Asset pricesfall belowintrinsic value. Capital markets slam shut, making it hard to issue securities debt. Defaults soar. Skepticism is high and faith is low, meaning only safe deals can or maybe none at all. No one considers improvement possible. No outcome seemstoo to happen. Everyone assumes thingswill get worseforever. Investors ignore the possibility of missing opportunity and worry about losingmoney. No one can think of a reason to buy. Sellers outnumber buyers. “Don’t try to catch a falling knife” takes the place of Pricesreach new lows. The mediafixate on this depressing trend. Investors become depressed and panicked. Security holders feel dumb and disillusioned. They realize they really understand the reasons behind the investmentsthey made. Thosewho abstained from buying (or who sold) feel validated and celebrated for their brilliance. Thosewho held give up and sell at depressed prices, adding the downward spiral. Implied prospective returns are sky-high. Risk is low. Investors should forgetabout the risk of losingmoney and worry about missing opportunity. This is the time to be aggressive! In the reverse of the “top” that results from the upswing of cycle,now we see that the nadir of psychology,a total inability to credit, minimum price, maximum potential returnand minimum risk all coincide at the bottom, when the last optimist throws in the The progressions outlined here are simplistic. In fact, they can see cartoonish depictions of the road to failure. But they are not imag exaggerated. It is absolutely logical that each event will bring on

in both directions . . . until an illogical extreme is reach cards collapses. The events don’t always occur in the same order,and not all of necessarily present in every market cycle.But these behaviors are real, they certainly are elements that rhyme in the markets from decad The first time an inexperienced investor lives through an upward cycle,the beginnings of the progression may seem logical, as the posit compound in a bull market or bubble. The fact that so much good feeling can end in lossescan come as a surprise. It is will seem so to the uninitiated, of course, because if the progr permitted to go on to extremes on the basis of errorsin judgm wouldn’t reach bull market tops to collapse from (or bear market recover from). ∾ On pages 19–21, I discussed the relationship between wherewe stand cycles and what that implies for prospective returns. This conceptual depiction indicates the relationship between cycle level and potential for return. It’s far from scientific, but everything I it’s right.

XIII HOW TO COPE WITH MARKET CYCLES What’s the key in all of this? To know wherethe pendulum o psychology and the cycle in valuation stand in their swings. To r to buy—and perhaps to sell—when too-positivepsychology and the willingness to assign too-high valuations cause pricesto soar to p levels. And to buy when downcast psychology and the desertion o valuation standards on the downside cause panicky investors to c bargains by selling, despite the low pricesthat result. The investor’s goal is to position capital so as to benefit developments. He wantsto have more invested when the market rises when it falls, and to own more of the thingsthat rise more or fall of the others. The objective is clear. The question is how to The first step is to decide how you will deal with the future. believe in economic and market forecasting, and in taking the such forecasts demand. Thus they investmore aggressively when forecasts call for favorable events, and vice versa. As I’ve made clear, I don’t believe in forecasting. Very few people know enough about what the futureholds for it to add to their retur record of most forecasters—in terms of both predicting events bette othersand having better investment performance than othersas a resul quite lackluster. A few people become famous in each period spectacular successes, but usually their next correct forecast doesn’t many years. In the absence of the ability to see the future, how ca portfolios for what lies ahead? I think much of the answer lies understanding wherethe market stands in its cycle and what that its futuremovements.As I wrote in The Most Important Thing, “we never know wherewe’re going, but we’d better have a good idea wherewe are.” To do that requires an understanding of the basic nature of general: what gives rise to their movements,what causes them to progr

toward peaks and troughs, and what causes them to retreat from extremes? We’ve touched on the key elements that concern us: the tendency of basic themes to repeatand of history to the tendency of thingsto rise and fall, especially those determined human nature, the way each development in a cycle has implications for the way the various cycles interact and influence each other, the role of psychology in pushing cyclical phenomena beyond levels, thus the tendency of cycles to go to extremes, their tendency to move from extremes back toward a midpoint, the regularity with whichthat movement continues past the midpoint, toward the opposite extreme. Theseare the generalities that affect cycles of all kinds.In addition, have to bear in mind the specific elements that influence the mark the economic and profit cycles that shape the investment environment the tendency of psychology to overreact to developments in environment, the way risk is considered non-existent and benign at some then enormous, inescapable and lethal at others, and the way market pricesreflect only positives and overstate them point, and then reflect only negatives and ignore all the positiv another. Theseare the fundamentals—the basicsregarding cycles in general specific ways they work in the markets—that we must perceive, atten obey. We must use the insights we garner to assesswherethe mark positioned, what that implies for its futuremovements,and what we shou do as a result. ∾ Equipped with a deep understanding of the above, we turn to figuring out wherewe stand in the cycle. What’s the key in all of this? To know wherethe pendulum of and the cycle in valuation stand in their swings. To refuseto buy— perhaps to sell—when too-positivepsychology and the willingness to assig too-high valuations cause pricesto soar to peak levels. And to buy downcast psychology and the desertion of valuation standards on the downside cause panicky investors to createbargains by selling despi

pricesthat prevail. As Sir John Templeton put it, “To buy when other despondently selling and sell when othersare greedily buying requi greatest fortitude and pays the greatest reward.” The upward movement of pricesfrom fair value to excess usually to the presence of some combination of important elements: generally good news, complacency regarding events, uniformly upbeat treatment by the media, the unquestioning acceptance of optimistic accounts, a decline in skepticism, a dearth of risk aversion, a wide-open credit market, and a positive general mood. Conversely,the collapse of pricesfrom fair value to bargain levelsis usually marked by some or all of the following: generally bad news, rising alarm regarding events, highly negative mediaaccounts, the wholesale acceptance of scare stories, a strong rise in skepticism, a significant increase in risk aversion, a credit market that has slammed shut, and a mood of general depression. The question is how we can tell wherethe market stands in Importantly, the elements that contribute to the market’s rise mani themselves via valuation metrics—p/e ratios on stocks, yieldson bond capitalization ratios on real estate, and cash flow multiples on buyo are elevated relative to historic norms. All of these thingsare low prospective returns. The reverse is true when a market colla asset pricesto bargain valuations. Thesethingscan be observed and quantified. In addition, our understanding of cycle positioning can be greatly an awareness of how investors are behaving. To respond to mark understand their message, one realization is more important than all other the risk in investing doesn’t come primarily from the economy, the companies, the securities, the stock certificates or the exchange buildings. comes from the behavior of the market participants.So do most opportunities for exceptional returns.

Wheninvestors act in a prudent manner, reflect risk aversion skepticism and restrain their positive emotions, security pricestend to reasonable relative to underlying value,making the market a safe place.On the other hand, when investors become euphoric, their overenthusiastic buying lifts pricesto dangerous levels. And when they’ despondent,their panicked selling takes pricesto bargain lows. Warren Buffett tells us, “The less prudence with whichothersconduct affairs, the greater the prudence with whichwe should conduct affairs.” Whenothersare euphoric, we should be terrified. And when are terrified, we should turn aggressive. It’s not what you buy that determines your results, it’s what you pa And what you pay—the security’s price and its relationship to intrinsic —is determined by investor psychology and the resulting behavior. The to being able to behave in a way that’s appropriate given the mark lies significantly in assessing psychology and the behavior of other have to know whether the market is red-hot and thus overpriced, thus a bargain. In “It Is What It Is” (March 2006)—andalso in The Most Im —I included what I calleda guide to market assessment. I can’t reason not to include it again here, or of a better substitute. these pointsare non-scientific, non-quantitative and non-quantifiable, and even somewhat jocular. But they should give you a sense for the watchout for: Economy: Vibrant Sluggish Outlook: Positive Negative Lenders: Eager Reticent Capital markets: Loose Tight Capital: Plentiful Scarce Terms: Easy Restrictive Interest rates: Low High Yield Spreads: Narrow Wide Investors: Optimistic Pessimistic Sanguine Distressed

And if you find your checkmarksare in the left-hand column, hold on to your walle Thesesorts of markers can tell us wherewe stand in the cycle an that’s likely to implyfor the future. Thus they help in what I call temperatureof the market.” Here’s how I described the process Important Thing:

If we are alert and perceptive, we can gaugethe behavior of th around us and from that judge what we should do. The essential ingredient here is inference, one of my favorite words. Everyone sees what happens each day, as reported in th media. But how many people make an effort to understand what th everyday events say about the psyches of market participants,th investment climate, and thus what should be done in response? Simply put, we must strive to understand the implications of going on around us. Whenothersare recklessly confident and buying aggressively, we should be highly cautious; when othersare frightened into inaction or panicked selling, we should become aggressive. So look around, and ask yourself: Are investors optimistic or pessimistic?Do the mediatalking heads say the markets should b piled into or avoided? Are novel investment schemes readily accepte or dismissed out of hand?Are securities offerings and fund opening being treated as opportunities to get rich or possible pitfalls credit cycle rendered capital readily available or impossible to o Are price/earnings ratios high or low in the context of history yield spreads tight or generous? All of these factors are important, and yet none of them entai forecasting. We can make excellent investment decisions on the basis of present observations, with no need to make guesses about th future. The key is to take note of thingslike these and let them tell what to do. Whilethe markets don’t cry out for actionalong these lines every day, they do at the extremes, when their pronouncements are highly important. At any time, lots of thingsare happening in the world, the ec investment environment. No one can study,parse,understand and incorporate all of them into investment decisions. And no one need try. At any different events occur in each cycle,and in a different sequence, different results. My point here is that not all the details are important. Rather, (a) figureout whichare the important ones, (b) make inferences about what going on from the important ones (and then perhaps consider as many less-important ones as we can), and (c) conclude from those inferences are the one or two thingsthat most characterizethe investment environmen and what actionthey call for. In other words, being attuned to cycli developments and their significance.

In particular, however, you can’t have the extremes of market cy without a departure of valuation metrics from their norms. Valu result—and thus symptomatic or indicative—of investor psychology. The psychological and emotional elements I’ve listed have their primary impact by convincing investors that past valuation standards have beco irrelevant and can be departed from. Wheninvestors are flyinghigh and making money, they find it easy to come up with convenient reaso assetsshould be untethered from the constraints of valuation norms. explanation usually begins with, “it’s different this time.” Watch ominous sign of the willing suspension of disbelief. Likewise, when pricescollapse in a crash,it’s usually because of an assumption the thingsthat supported value in the past can be trusted to work So the key to understanding wherewe stand in the cycle depends formsof assessment: The first is totally quantitative: gauging valuations. This is appropriate starting point, for if valuations aren’tout of line with the market cycle is unlikely to be highly extended in And the second is essentially qualitative: awareness of what’s around us, and in particular of investor behavior. Importantly, possible to be disciplined even in observing these largely nonquantitative phenomena. The key questions can be boiled down to two: how are thingspri how are investors around us behaving? Assessing these two elements— consistentlyand in a disciplined manner—can be very helpful. The will give us a sense for wherewe stand in the cycle. In closing on this subject, I want to repeatsomething I’ve be even the best of temperature-taking can’t tell us will happen next tendencies. Since market cycles vary from one to the next in terms of the pace and duration of their fluctuations, they’re not regular enou us to be sure what’ll happen next on the basis of what has from a given point in the cycle,the market is capable of movi direction: up, flat or down.

But that doesn’t mean all three are equally likely. Where we influences the tendencies or probabilities, even if it does not deter developments with certainty. All other thingsbeing equal,when the market high in its cycle,a downward correction is more likely than continued and vice versa.It doesn’t have to work out that way, of course, safer bet. Assessing our cycle position doesn’t tell us what will happ just what’s more and less likely. But that’s a lot. ∾ The best way to teach the recognition of cyclical excesses is throu examples of how it can be done at the extremes, whereit matte over the following pages I’m going to review the formation of two bubbles and the crash that followed the second of them. Even the were different, but each of these three market events illustrates the of assessing the market’s temperature. First, let’s review the meteoric rise of stocks in the late 19 2000, and especially the formation of the Internet bubble. What shou alert investor have noted during that time? In the decade of the 1990s, the U.S. economy enjoyed peacetime expansion in its history. In December 1996, when the S&P 500 index of equities stood at Fed Chairman Alan Greenspan asked, “Howdo we know when irratio exuberance has unduly escalated asset values?” But he was never

from again on this subject, even as the S&P more than doubled of 1527 in 2000. In 1994, Prof. Jeremy Siegel of the Wharton School publis Stocks for the Long Run, in whichhe pointed out that there been a long period of time in whichstocks had failed to bonds, cash and inflation. Whereas researchers at the University of Chicago had earlier that the normal returnon U.S. equities was in the vicinity in the 1990sthe average returnon the S&P was nearly 20%. The better stocks performed, the more capital investors allocated them. This was especially true of technology stocks, whichwere the clear market leaders. More tech stocks were addedto equity indices like the S&P meaning index and quasi-index investors had to buy more of them— whichmade them go up—which attracted still more capital to them. was a classic “virtuous circle,” and no one could imagine The absence of earnings at most “new-economy” companies elimin any requirementthat their stocks should sell at reasonable price/e ratios. In the latter stagesof the bubble, the pricesof some dot-com increased several hundred percent on the day of their initial offerings. To be willing to buy the new stocks at their prices, buyers who thought about it would have to have either (a) the companies’ founders had been happyto sell the stocks fraction of their true value or (b) the founders knew less about stocks’ value than the buyers did. Both of these conclusions were tenuous. In order to share in this miracle—and to avoid the pain of othersprofited—investors took part in IPOs of companies that had earnings (and in some cases no revenues) and about whose busine models they knew little or nothing. The year 1999 saw the publication of Dow 36,000, a book whose authors, JamesGlassman and KevinHassett, argued that because were so low in risk (see Siegel on page 217), they needn’t a risk premium as they historically had. That meanttheir pricesshould immediately rise to levelsfrom whichthey would offer appropriatel reduced prospective returns. Thus the Dow Jones Industrial Average deserved to more than triple from just over 10,000 at the time. The p/e ratio on the S&P 500—fueled by rampaging growth technology stocks—reached a high of 33 during the tech-stock fully double the post-war norm and the highest valuation in history up to that point in time.

So what ingredients actually were present? good economic news, favorable articles and books, carefree, risk-oblivious behavior on the part of investors, unusually strong investment returns, extremely high valuations relative to history, widespread willingness to pay pricesthat could not be justified analytically,and belief in a perpetual motion machine that would never stop. This last point is particularly important. Everybubble starts with a gra truth, as I noted earlier. But the import and profit potential of overestimated in bubbles; disbelief is suspended; and it is wide that the gains can roll on forever. Certainly an objective temperature-taker could have known the pieces in place for a substantial bubble that was likely to fall to earth of phenomena as I described them in “bubble.com” (January 2000 Webvan Group, whichstarted in business in 1999, had sales million and a $350,000 profit in the September quarter. The currently values it at $7.3 billion. On December 9, VA Linuxwent public at 30 and soared to $239, for a market value of $9.5 billion, half that of date, the company’s 1999 sales were $17.7 million and it had lost million (versus Apple’s profit of $600 million in the most months). Because the price/earnings ratios of Internet companies are so —usually negative—one may be forced to look to the price/s order to speak about valuation. Red Hat, for example, sells at about 1,000 times its annualized revenues in the August 1999 quarter. Among non-Internet tech companies, Yahoo! is worth$119 billion than General Motors and Ford together. At the current stock $432, its p/e ratio on 1999 estimated earnings is just over 1,000. Under these unusual circumstances, the Wall StreetJournal wrote o December 10, 1999 that “stock valuations take on an unusually la importance in gauging a business’s performance.” In other words— the absence of other signs—people must look to the share price f indication of how the company is doing. Isn’t that backwards? I old days, investors figured out how the business was doing and th set the share price to reflect it.

In this valuation parameter vacuum, a “lottery ticket mentality” seemsto govern the purchase decision. The model for investm the tech and dot-com companies isn’t the likelihood of a 20% o annual returnbased on projected earnings and p/e ratios, but a a 1,000% gain based on a concept. The pitch may be, “We’re for first-round financing for a company valued at $30 million think can IPO in two years at $2 billion.” Or maybe it’s “ be priced at $20. It may end the day at $100 and be a months.” Would you play? Couldyou stand the risk of saying no and wrong? The pressure to buy can be immense. Therehave always been ideas, stocks and IPOs that produced great profits. Yet the pressure to participate wasn’t as great as it because in the past the winners made millions, not billions, a years,not months. The upside in the deals that’ve worked s been 100-to-1 (give or take a zero). With that kind of potential, ( upside becomes irresistible and (b) it doesn’t take a very h probability of success to justify the investment. I have said in that while the market is usually driven by fear and greed, the biggest motivator is the fear of missing out. Neverwas th as today. This only intensifies the pressure to join in and c out on that limb of risk. The bottom line is that in an extreme bubble like this on investor doesn’t have to make fine distinctions.All you have to be is identify nutty behavior when you see it. To me—as an obser in detached fashion from the sidelines, ratherthan someone with skin tech-investing game—the events of the tech/Internet craze had the of a Hans Christian Andersen tale. Thosewho participated in it so roll on that no one would step forward to say the emperor Developments like those just described were signs of the mass hysteria part of every bubble. Thosewho were enriched by the performance of equities in the especially by the tech and Internet stocks—took it to foretell prosperity and wealth creation, and they responded to the returns enjoyed by ratcheting up their expectations for futurereturns. (As Munger quotes the ancient Greekstatesman and oratorDemosthenes, what each man wishes, that he also believes to be true.”) But concluded that euphoria-driven buying in the 1990shad overpaid for and borrowed from futureperformance, with highly negative implication just took a couple of years for them to be proved right.

The eventual result in 2000–02 was (a) the first three-year decline stock market since 1929–31, with a 49% drop in the S&P 500 from bottom (ignoring income), (b) massive declines in tech stocks, of 100%on many Internet and e-commerce stocks. ∾ Usually one would expect that the painful deflating of such had been driven by excesses of optimism and credulousness—would serious instructional impact, meaning it would take a decad another such bubble to arise. But that was not to be the case. important, I’m going to repeatonce more what John Kenneth Galbraith said about the learning of financial lessons: Contributing to . . . euphoria are two further factors time or in past times.The first is the extreme brevity of th memory. In consequence, financial disaster is quickly forgotte further consequence, when the same or closely similar circum occur again,sometimes in only a few years,they are hailedby a n often youthful, and always supremely self-confident generation as a brilliantly innovative discovery in the financial and largereconomic world. The events that only a few years later beganto lead to the su mortgage boom—adequately described earlier in this book—provided example of the way cyclical excesses are built on error: As I wrote on page 119, some U.S. leaders decided that only come from increased home ownership. The federal agencies charged with issuing mortgages got the and ramped up the availability of mortgages. Declining interest rates made mortgages—and thus home ownership— increasinglyaffordable. Increased home affordability and increased mortgage availability allowed home buying to increase and the demand for homes This increased demand caused the pricesof homes to increa substantially. The canard that “home pricesonly go up” becam accepted truth, feeding back to produce still more demand for The further platitude that “therecan never be a nationwide wave mortgage defaults” caused mortgage backed securities to be primecandidates for investment, particularly by financial institutions. Wall Streetcame forward with a model for securitizing prosaic, home mortgages into tranched collateralized debt obligations—the next

high-return, low-risk thing. The construction and selling of CDOs and other mortgage securities (MBS) addedgreatly to bank profits. With relaxed regulations allowing banksto employ materially greate leverage, large amounts of capital were available for investment equity required for MBS creation. The meteoric growth of MBS packaging created rapidly increa demand for the essential raw material in the process: newly mortgages. In order to expand the volume of mortgages they issued, novel ways to increase their appeal to borrowers: interest-only that minimized monthly payments by eliminating the traditional requirementthat the principal balance be paid down; adjustable-ra mortgages that allowed borrowers to benefit from the ultra-low rates at the short end of the yield curve; and, most importantly, prime” mortgages (sometimes called“liar loans”) that didn’trequire applicants to document income and employment. With sub-prime mortgages being packaged into securities and sold onward, as opposed to being retained as in the past, lender shifted from borrowers’ creditworthiness to loan volume. With lender being paid fees simply for making loans—and able to sell them immediately and thus retain no risk of default—there was no them to worryabout the creditworthiness of their borrowers. Clearly gave them perverse incentives. (Incentives like these—which allow participants to engage in pro-risk behavior without having about the consequences—were described in the Global Financial as creating “moral hazard,” a term that came into widespread it’s heard less often these days, the concept survives, and it remain dangerous.) The key to the purported success of sub-prime mortgage backe securities lay in “financial engineering” performed by “quants” Ph.D.’s, many of them in their first jobs. They modeled risk based flawed assumption that mortgage defaults would remain uncorr and benign as in the past. The creation of large amounts of sub-prime MBS meantthere was of business for the ratingagencies whose imprimatur was essent these profitable assignmentswould go to the agency that provid highest rating. This led to ratings shopping and created incentives, this time supporting widespread ratings inflation. Thesetrendscaused the ratingagencies to issue many thousands of triple-A ratings to mortgage backed securities (as compared U.S. companies enjoying triple-A ratings at the time).Rating

personnel clearly lacked the ability to fully understand the they were assessing. Banks and other financial institutions bought huge amounts of abetted by “risk-management” techniques such as Valueat Risk relied heavily on the short, benign history and overly genero and thus vastlyunderstated the risk buyers were assuming. All of the aboveoccurred in and was facilitated by risk-obliviou attitudes characterized by risk-is-gonerhetoric (see page 120). So what was at the root of the formation of this bubble? Ac “NowIt’s All Bad?” (September 2007): . . . a standard combination that proved perfectly in underlying greed, good returns in the up-leg of the cycle, euphoria and complacency, a free-and-easy credit market, Wall Street’s inventiveness and salesmanship, and investors’ naiveté. The resulting developments that fed the upswing are clear: a huge decrease in risk aversion, and thus the disappearance skepticism, the acceptance of sweeping positive generalizations regarding homes mortgages, excessive faith in new tools like financial engineering and risk management, and widespread blindness to the impact of improper incentives on participants in the process. In addition to short memories, psychological excesses and logical the bubble that arose in mortgage backed securities was abetted additional factors: Because this new bubble arose in mortgage-land—apart of the markets completely separate from that whichhad been visited by tech and Internet bubble—the fixed income investors and financ institutions it appealed to were ones who hadn’t been affected by the other, and hadn’t learned from it. The terrible recentperformance of equities had so discouraged investors—and interest rates brought low by the dovish Fed

diminished the yieldsavailable on fixed income investments—that investors gave up on obtaining strong returns from stocks That rendered them highly susceptible to the promise of a returnwithout risk: mortgage backed securities. This is a good time for a valuable aside. Falling for the su asset that will provide returnwithout risk, or what I call the “silv (dating back to The Lone Ranger, a 1950sTV Western about a gunshots never missed)—isone of investors’ greatest recurring failings. exemplifies “whatevery man wishes,” as Demosthenes said, but it make sense.If there were a silver bullet: Why would the purveyor offer it to you ratherthan buying And wouldn’t everyone else buy it and drive up its price to whereit’s no longer a sure thing? I’ve seen dozens of silver bullets touted over the course of career. Not one has proved out. No investment strategy or tactic deliver a high returnwithout risk, especially to buyers lacking investing skill. Outstandinginvestment results can only come from exceptional skill (or perhaps in isolated moments from good luck). As I said earlier, bubbles generally spring from a grain of all taken too far, and their eventual puncturing brings the greatest investment world. Beliefin a silver bullet underlies many bubbles. The that investors are willing to swallow a promise of returnwitho infallible indicator that skepticism is in short supply, psychology is overheated, and the subject asset is overestimated and thus probably overpriced. Watch out for it, and resist if you can. By definition, comes to bubbles, few people do. I would sum up the key conclusion this way: The sub-prime mo bubble arose from broad acceptance that phenomena would work that had never before been seen in action. The analysis of investment vehic entail (a) the application of skepticism and conservative assumptions examination over a long history that includes some tryingtimes.Thes were clearly missing. In fact, most bubbles, if not all, are characterized by the un acceptance of thingsthat have never held true in the past; of valuations are dramatically out of line with historic norms; and/or of techniques and tools that haven’t been tested. ∾

The sub-prime mortgage bubble demonstrated an extremely important principle in actionthat I haven’t touched upon before now. The investment environment—and the performance of investment techniques instruments—is not immutable. Rather, as I’ve said many times with regar cycles, these thingsare affected by the involvement of people. Thereis no such thing as a market that is separate from—a —the people who make it up. The behavior of the people in changes the market. Whentheir attitudes and behavior change, the will change. In the case at hand: market forceschanged the participants’ motivations, as just these changed motivations changed their behavior, and their changed behavior clearly determined the results. An essential progression was at work, as is clear to see: History was taken as saying there couldn’t be a nationwide mortgage defaults. Acceptance of this benign history caused vast sums to be mortgage backed securities. It permitted the securities to be structured aggressively. It also caused ratingagencies to extrapolate the benign experi grant high ratings. Most importantly,the strong demand for mortgage backed securi created a need for the raw material—mortgage loans—and led eagerness to issue them that caused lending standards to deterio Thesedevelopments, taken together, virtually guaranteed that there wouldbe a nationwide wave of mortgage defaults. As I mentioned earlier, because of the impact of careless —the import of whichwent largely unnoticed and unremarkedat the the default experience on mortgages issued from the late 1990sthrou turned out to be much worsethan it historically had been; worsethan security structurers and ratingagencies had thought possible; and wors buyers’ models had said it would be. Ignoring this possibility significant contributionto the creation of the sub-prime mortgage bubb occurrence caused the resulting crisis. The key is to understand that the behavior of investors can alt changing the results that investors can expect the market to reflects George Soros’s theory of reflexivity:

In situations that have thinking participants,the participants’ . . distorted viewscan influence the situation to whichthey relate because false viewslead to inappropriate actions. (“Soros: Genera Theory of Reflexivity,” Financial Times, October 26, 2009) People tryingto understand how thingswork in the economic and fin worlds should take this lesson very much to heart. ∾ All it takes for the perpetual motion machine to grind to a one or two assumptions and the operation of some general rules Interest rates can go up as well as down. Platitudes can fail to hold. Improper incentives can lead to destructive behavior. ∾ Finally in discussing how to detectand respond to market cycle want to returnonce more to the widespread panic that followed the bankruptcy filing by Lehman Brothers in September 2008. Although the sub-prime mortgage crisis originated in a small corner financial and investment world, the impact was soon felt widely, by the financial institutions that had underestimated the risk in mort backed securities and thus invested too heavily in them. As a threat to these essential institutions, the impact metastasized to the bond markets in all countries—and then to economies all around in the form of the Global Financial Crisis. Thus, as I described earlier, money market funds and commerc to be guaranteed by the U.S. government. A number of prom financial institutions failed or had to be bailedout/rescued/absorbed. No knew how far the carnage would spread. The equity and debt collapsed. Now the generalizing was on the negative side: “the finan system could totally melt down” in a vicious circle without Since the generalizations were on the downside, the error-making ma went into reverse. No greed, only fear. No optimism, only pessimism. tolerance, only risk aversion. No ability to see positives, only nega willingness to interpret thingspositively, only negatively. No ability to imagine good outcomes, only bad. Thus we reached the day on whic the discussion mentioned back on pages 131–132, in whichthe pension head was unable or unwilling to accept that any assumption regar possible defaults could be conservative enough. What was the essential observation? Here’s what I wrote in “T Negativism” (October 2008): Contrarianism—doing the opposite of what othersdo, or “leaning against the wind”—is essential for investment success. But as th credit crisis reached a peak last week,people succumbed to the w ratherthan resisting. I foundvery few who were optimistic; most were pessimistic to some degree. Some became genuinely depressed—even a few great investors I know. Increasingly negative tales o coming meltdown were exchanged via email. No one applied skepticism, or said “that horror story’s unlikely to be true.” P fed on itself. People’s only concern was bullet-proofing their portfolios to get through the coming collapse, or raising e to meet redemptions. The one thing they weren’t doing last week w

making aggressive bids for securities. So pricesfell and fell, several pointsat a time—the old expression is “gapped down.” The key—as usual—was to become skeptical of what “everyone” was saying and doing. One mighthave said, “Sure, the negativ may turn out to be true, but certainly it’s priced into the m there’s little to be gained from betting on it. On the o turns out not to be true, the appreciation from today’s depress will be enormous. I buy!” The negative story may have looked compelling, but it’s the positive story—which few believed—that held, and still holds,the greater potential for profit. At this market cycle extreme, all the news truly was negative . certainly not imaginary. The only questions I received were “Howfar go?” and “What will be the effects?” Giventhat asset pricesreflected but abjectpessimism regarding these things—I’d say near-suicidal thinking— the key to profiting lay in recognizing that even in the face of news and a very poor outlook, pessimism can be overdone, and thus can become too cheap. It was the excessiveness of the prevailing pessimism that led me “The Limits to Negativism” at the credit market’s low ebb in In it I pointed out, as mentioned in the chapter on attitu the superior investor’s essential skepticism “calls for pessimism when optimism is excessive. But it also calls for optimism when pessimism excessive.” That variety of skepticism was totally lacking in the darkest days, of course. Shortly after Lehman’s bankruptcy filing on September 15, 2008, Bruce Karshand I reached the conclusion that (a) no one could know how financial institution meltdown would go, but (b) negativity was certa rampant and very possibly excessive, and assetslooked terribly cheap Thinking strategically, we decided that if the financial worldende no one could rule out—it wouldn’t matter whether we’d bought the worlddidn’tend and we hadn’t bought, we would have failed job. So we bought debt aggressively. Oaktree invested more than a dollars a week over the fifteen weeks from September 15 throu the year. Some days we thought we were going too fast, and some days slow; that probably meantwe had it about right. The worlddidn’tend; the vicious cycle of financial institution implosion stopped with Lehman Brothers; the capital markets reopened; the financial institutions came life; debt was again able to be refinanced; bankruptcies turned out few relative to history; and the assetswe bought appreciated subst short, paying heed to the cycle was rewarded.

∾ Whilewe’re reviewing the climate in late 2008, this is an appropriate a discussion of investor behavior on the way to, and at, mark First, what is a bottom? It’s the point when the lowest pricesof are reached. Thus a bottom can be viewed as the day the sells, or the last day on whichsellers predominate relative to whatever reason, it’s the last day pricesgo down, and thus the their nadir. (Of course these definitions are highly exaggerated. The expression “a bottom”—like “a top”—describes a period of time, Thus the phrase “the last day” is mostly a figureof speech.) pricesrise, since there are no holders left to capitulate and sell, or buyers now want to buy more strongly than the sellers want to The question I want to touch on here is, “When should on made reference in earlier chapters to “falling knives,” whichconst important concept. Whena market is cascading downward, investors often be heard to say, “We’re not going to try to catch a fallin other words, “The trend is downward and there’s no way to know stop, so why should we buy before we’re sure the bottom has What I think they’re really saying is, “We’re scared—in particula buying before the decline has stopped, and thus of looking going to wait until the bottom has been reached, the dust has settle uncertainty has been resolved.” But hopefully by now I’ve made it abun clear that when the dust has settled and investors’ nerves have stead bargains will be gone. At Oaktree, we strongly reject the idea of waiting for the bo buying. First, there’s absolutely no way to know when the bottom has reached. There’s no neon sign that lights up. The bottom can recognized only after it has been passed, since it is defined before the recovery begins. By definition, this can be identif after the fact. And second, it’s usually during market slides that you can quantities of the thing you want, from sellers who are throwing towel and while the non-knife-catchers are hugging the sidelines. once the slide has culminated in a bottom, by definition there sellers left to sell, and during the ensuing rally it’s buyers predominate. Thus the selling dries up and would-be buyers growing competition.

We beganto buy distressed debt immediately after Lehman filed for bankruptcy protection in mid-September 2008 as described on page 235, we continued through year-end, as priceswent lower and lower. By quarter of 2009, other investors had collected themselves, caught on values that were available, and gathered some capital for investment the motivated sellers done selling and buying having begun, them to buy in size without pushing up prices. Like so many other thingsin the investment worldthat mightbe tried on the basis of certitude and precision, waiting for the bottom to great example of folly. So if targeting the bottom is wrong, buy? The answer’s simple: when price is belowintrinsic value.What if price continues downward? Buy more, as now it’s probably an even great bargain. All you need for ultimate success in this regard is intrinsic value,(b) the emotional fortitude to persevere, and (c) even have your estimate of value proved correct. ∾ Here’s how the prominent investment indices did the following year. returns that were available in 2009 show the importance of having a cycle at a negative extreme and having bought in (or through) the accompanying chaos. Experiencing a mark-to-market loss in the down of a cycle isn’t fatal in and of itself, as long as you hold throu upward part as well. It’s converting that downward fluctuation into a permanent loss by selling out at the bottom that’s really terrible. Thus understanding cycles and having the emotional and financial wherewithal needed to live through them is an essential ingre investment success. ∾ Before I declare victory in the Global Financial Crisisand state firmly that the success my colleagues and I enjoyed in this cycle wasn’t inevitable. That’s because—reflecting Elroy Dimson’s theme—the good outcome we got wasn’t the only outcome that could materialized. I’m convinced that if Hank Paulson, Tim Geithner and Bernanke hadn’t acted when they did, or if they had acted differently, their actions hadn’t been as successful as they were, a financial replay of the Great Depression absolutely could have occurred. In that our actions wouldn’t have been cause for celebration. I fear that people may look back at the decline of 2008 an that followed and conclude that declines can always be depended recouped promptly and easily, and thus there’s nothing to worr down-cycles. But I think those are the wrong lessons from the the outcome that actually occurred was so much better than some of “alternative histories” (as Nassim Nicholas Taleb calls them)that could have occurred instead. And if those incorrect lessons are the ones that as I believe they may have been, then they’re likely to bring on increases the amplitude of another dramatic boom/bust cycle someday, one with more serious and long-lastingramifications for investors and of society. But thingsdid break for us in the recovery from the Crisis, an “long” investors. How you deal with cycles is one of the most important thingsin Cycles will happen to you. What you do in response is key.

But I want to comp of the actions investors can take to improve performance by other component of portfolio management: asset selection. Asset selection consists of identifying markets, market sectors an individual assetsthat will do better or worsethan the rest, and over- and underweighting them in portfolios. The higher an asset’s price is intrinsic value,the less well it should be expected to do (all

equal), and vice versa.The key prerequisite for superior performance regard is above-average insight into the asset’s intrinsic value futurechanges in that value,and the relationship between its intrinsic and its current market price. All investors who follow a given asset have (or should have) op regarding its intrinsic value.The market price of the asset reflects consensus of those opinions, meaning investors collectively have set the price. That’s wherebuyers and sellers agree to transact. The buye because they think it’s a smart investment at the current price, and sell because they think it’s fully priced or overpriced there. What do about the accuracy of those views? Theoretical—The efficient market hypothesis states that all availa information is incorporated into prices“efficiently,” so that the assetsare fair and investors can’t “beat the market” by choosing them. Logical—What we’re talking about is the ability to make those judgments better than the average investor and thus achieve above average performance. Yet the one thing we know for sure is average, all investors are average. Thus logic tells us they can’t above-average judgments. Empirical—Performance studies show that very few investors are consistentlymore right than othersabout those judgments. Most investors do worsethan the markets, especially after the subtraction transaction costs, management fees and expenses. That’s the reason the rising popularity of passive index investing. That’s not to say no one beats the market. Lots of people but usually no more than would be the case under an assumption randomness. A few do it more consistentlythan randomnesswould and some of those become famous. The essential ingredient—superior into intrinsic value—is what gives them that ability. I call it “seco thinking”: the ability to think differently from the consensus and bette I’m not going to go any further regarding intrinsic value,the rel price to value,or second-level thinking, since they’re all covered The Most Important Thing. But the bottom line is that the super who’scapable of second-level thinking—is able to pick more outpe assetsthan underperformers, and thus to investmore in the former and the latter. The recipefor superior asset selection is that simple. And what is the hallmark of that superiority?Asymmetrical results. The investor who has no skill at selection has the same ratio of losersas the market. Thus he does well when the market does well,

poorly when it does poorly: An investor with negative skill at selection picks proportionally mo than winners and thus does worsethan the market, both when it goes when it goes down, as shown below. In other words, his distribution is shifted to the left of the market’s: But the skillful selector has a better ratio of winners to los market; he is able to investmore in his winners than his loser winners are more successful than his losersare unsuccessful.

The holdings of a habitually aggressive investor who is capab superior selection will go up more than the market when the up and may go down more than the market when it goes down. margin of superiority on the upside will exceed his degree on the downside, as it comes from his ability to select upside potential without entailing commensurate downside risk. result he’ll do better than the market when it goes up, but not when the market goes down as his aggressiveness would sugge an example of the asymmetry that marksthe superior investor: Likewise, the habitually defensive investor with superior selection will do better than the market when it goes down, but his selection will keep him from underperforming in rising markets to full extentthat his defensiveness alone would suggest. His skill selection enables him to find defensive assetsthat have upside participation that is disproportionate to their downside risk. It an asymmetrical distribution as well:

Both of the investors with superior selection skill—the aggressive on the defensive one—exhibit asymmetry relative to the market. That both have performance distributions that are biased favorably. Both upside potential that is disproportional to their downside risk (altho different ways). That’s how alpha in asset selection manifests itself Finally, the investor who is neither habitually aggressive nor ha defensive—but who possesses skill at both cycle positioning and asset selection—correctly adjusts market exposure at the right time and asymmetrical performance that comes from a better-than-average winners to losers. This is the best of all worlds: Almost anyone can make money when the market rises and los when it falls, and almost anyone can have the same ratio of winn

as the market overall. It takes superior skill to improve in produce the asymmetry that marksthe superior investor. Please note that in this discussion I have separated skill at cy from skill at asset selection. This bifurcation is somewhat artificial.
