XVI THE CYCLE IN SUCCESS The important lesson is that—especially in an interconnected, informed world—everything that produces unusual profitabilitywill attract incremental capital until it becomes overcrowded and f institutionalized, at whichpoint its prospective risk-adjusted return will move toward the mean (or worse). And, correspondingly, thingsthat perform poorly for a while eventually will become so cheap—dueto their relative depreciation and the lack of investor interest—that they’ll be primed to outperform. Cycles like these hold the key to success in investin trees that everyone is assuming will grow to the sky. Hopefully you’re now equipped for the task of recognizing, responding to cycles. That can contribute substantially to your inves success. But as Peter Bernstein said, even the best investors won’tbe successful all the time. Understanding that is an important part of to live with the effort. Success, like the other thingsmentioned in comes and goes. In fact, over the course of my career, I’ve detected a cy large degree, the ebb and flow of success—like the other cycles described—stems from the role played by human nature. And, once each development in the cycle leads to the next. I’ve long held the I mentioned back on page 34—and it’s been strongly reinforced over years of involvement with distressed debt and distressed companies—that “success carries within itself the seeds of failure, and failure success.” Peter Kaufman, Charlie Munger’s biographer and the CEO of Glenair, exceptional producer of aerospace components, describes the workings dialectical materialism as follows: “As any system growstoward its maximum or peak efficiency, it will develop the very internal contr and weaknesses that bring about its eventual decayand demise” (his essay

#49: “The Perpetual See-Saw,” 2010). This captures the process that guarantees that success will prove cyclical. The Role of Human Nature Another way I put it is that “success isn’t good for most peop success can change people, and usually not for the better. people think they’re smart. That’s fine as far as it goes, negative ramifications. Success also tends to make people richer, lead to a reduction in their level of motivation. In investing there’s a complex relationship between humility an confidence. Since the greatest bargains are usually foundamong thing are undiscovered or disrespected, to be successful an investor enough confidence in his judgment to adopt what DavidSwensen, the successful head of Yale’s high-performing endowment,describes as “uncomfortably idiosyncratic portfolios, whichfrequently appear downright imprudent in the eyes of conventional wisdom” (PioneeringPortfolio Management, 2000). By definition, pronounced bargain pricesare most to be foundamong thingsthat conventional wisdom dismisses, that most investors uncomfortable, and whose merits are hard to comp Investing in them requires considerable inner strength. Whenone of those positions initially fails to rise as the investor or perhaps goes in the opposite direction—the investor has to confidence to hold on to his position or even add to it. He decline as a sure “sell” signal; in other words, it can’t be that the market knows more than he does. But, on the other hand, the investor also has to know his limitatio assume he’s infallible. He has to understand that no one knows the macro futureholds.Whilehe’s likely to have opinions regarding the futurecourse of economies, markets and interest rates, he should acknowledge that they’re not necessarily correct. And, counter to he mustn’t always assume that he’s right and the market’s wron hold or add without limitation and without rechecking his facts and reasoning. That’s hubris. As successes accrue, it’s common for people to conclude tha And after making a lot of money in a strongly rising mark they’ve got investing mastered. Their faith increases in their own opini and instinct. Their investing comes to reflect less self-doubt, mean think less about the possibility of being wrong and worryless about the of loss. This can cause them to no longer insist on the full marg

that gave rise to their earlier successes. This is the reason for and most important investment adages: “don’t confuse brainswith a market.” The plain truth is, there’s little of value to be learned from su who are successful run the risk of overlookingthe fact that they were or that they had help from others. In investing, success teaches making money is easy, and that they don’t have to worryabout risk— particularly dangerous lessons. They may conclude that the small opportunity that gave them their big winner is infinitely scalable, whichmost are not. And many people— including investors who became famous for having had a singl conclude that they can branch out into any number of other fields intelligence that produced that first epic success must be broadly appli Factors like these make investment success hard to replicate, meaning can prove cyclical ratherthan serial.In fact, ratherthan implythat another coming, one success may in and of itself make a second one quote Henry Kaufman, formerly Salomon Brothers’ chief economist: “The are two kinds of people who lose a lot of money: those who those who know everything” (“Archimedes on Wall Street,” Forbes, Octo 19, 1998). It’s not for nothing that there are famous jinxes, such as sh cover of Sports Illustrated or Forbes magazine. A cover appearance the result of a singular accomplishment that may have resulted from break, a unique, non-replicable opportunity,or the bearing of Or the good outcomes that land people on a magazine cover—inc successful investors lauded by Forbes—may cause them to become confident and cocksure, and less disciplined and hardworking . . a formula for success. The Role of Popularity One of the principal ways in whichsuccess carries the seeds of failure is through increased popularity. I wrote just before that most often foundamong those thingsthat are hard to comprehend, uncomfortable and easilydismissed by the crowd. Short-term investment performance is largely a popularity contest, and most bargains simple reason that they haven’t yet been taken up by the herd popular. On the contrary, assetsthat have performed well are usually that have gained in popularity because of their obvious merit and become high-priced.

Let’s think about investment strategies. It’s essential to grasp that nothing will work forever: no approach, rule or process can outperform all First, most securities and approaches are right for certain environments parts of the cycle,and wrong for others. And second, past succe itself render futuresuccess less likely. In the 1960s, when the idea of investing in stocks was fir popularity among Americans, the emphasis was on industry leaders called“blue-chip” securities. Small-capitalization stocks were largely overlooked at first, but eventually they were noticed and bought. This them to do better than large-caps. Whenpeople noticed small-cap stock superior returns in this “catch-up” phase, their buying produced for them . . . until it caused them to be fully priced that point, interest rotated to large-caps, whichthen regained the lead. Likewise, growth and tech stocks did far better than more-mundane stocks in the late 1990s. This divergence reached a maximum growth stocks outperforming value stocks that year by almost points. But growth stocks’ dramatic outperformance rendered them overpriced, and when the stock market corrected in 2000–02, they lost more than value stocks, whichpreviously had languished cheap. In other words, “outperformance” is just another word for one thi appreciating relative to another. And, clearly, that can’t go on Regardless of how great its merits may be, “a” is unlikely to more valuable than “b.” That means if “a” keeps appreciating relati there has to be a point at whichit will become overvalued relati just when the last person gives up on “b” because it’s been perfo poorly and jumpsto “a,” it will be time for “b” (now compelling relative to “a”) to outperform. Strong forcescreatea tendency for strategies, investors or investme management firms that succeed for a while to cease doing so. I abovethat most ideas aren’tinfinitely scalable. One essential truth about investing is that, generally speaking, good results will bring more mone “hot” money managers and strategies, and if allowed to grow unch more money will bring bad performance. In the mid-2000s, convertible arbitrage rapidly gained in popularit Investors without a view on the prospects for a given stock were buy bondsconvertible into it, as long as they were able to short the shares in an appropriate “hedge ratio” (see my memo “A Case 2005). Convertible arbitrageursreported outstanding risk-adjusted returns all kinds of market environments . . . until so much mone competitors were attracted to the strategy that no one could find posit attractively priced as those of the past.

The important lesson is that—especially in an interconnected, inf world—everything that produces unusual profitabilitywill attract incre capital until it becomes overcrowded and fully institutionalized, at point its prospective risk-adjusted returnwill move toward the mean (or worse). And, correspondingly, thingsthat perform poorly for a while eventual will become so cheap—dueto their relative depreciation and the investor interest—that they’ll be primed to outperform. Cycles the key to success in investing, not trees that everyone is assum to the sky. It’s all a matter of ebb and flow. In investing, thingswork un Or as Ajit Jain of Berkshire Hathaway told me about investing the “it’s easy until it isn’t.” Cheap small-caps outperform until they reach the point wherethey’re longer cheap. Trend-following or momentum investing—staying with the winners— worksfor a while. But eventually rotation and buying the over as the winning strategy. “Buying the dips” lets investors take advantage of momentary weakn up until the time when a majorproblem surfaces (or the marke no longer recovers), causing price declines to be followed price declines, not quick rebounds. Riskyassetsoutperform—coming from valuations wherethey were excessively penalized for their riskiness—until they’re priced more safe assets. Then they underperform until they once again offer adequ risk premiums. The bottom line is clear: nothing worksforever. But it’s essential recognize that when everyone becomes convinced that something will keep working forever, that’s the very time when it’ll become certain not “In investing, everything that’s important is counter-intuitive, and everything that’s obvious to everyone is wrong.” Perhaps the greatest example of the influence of popularity (in seen in 1979, and few investors who were around at the time have it. On August 13 of that year, after almost a decade of performance on the part of stocks, Business Week magazine publi story entitled “The Deathof Equities.” Its conclusion—that stocks were for—was based on the opposite of everything recommended in this The article cited a litany of reasons why the poor performance would continue unabated:

Sevenmillion people had given up on investing in stocks. Manyother formsof investment had done better. Pension funds were turning to “hard assets” like gold. Inflation had sapped corporations’ ability to increase profits It went on: Even institutions that have so far remained in the financial market are pouring money into short-term investmentsand such “alternate equity” investmentsas mortgage-backed paper, foreign securities, venture capital, leases, guaranteed insurance contracts, indexed b stock options, and futures. And here was its conclusion: Today, the old attitude of buying solid stocks as a c one’s life savings and retirement has simply disappeared. Says a young U.S. executive: “Have you been to an American stockho meeting lately? They’re all old fogies. The stock market is wherethe action’s at.” In short, what “The Deathof Equities” said was that stocks had be unpopular that they would never do well again.It takes a highly first-level thinker to conclude that poor past performance has led unpopularity today, whichimplies poor performance tomorrow. Rath insightful second-level thinker says poor past performance has led unpopularity today, whichimplies low pricestoday, whichin turn good performance tomorrow. “The Deathof Equities” was published just a few years before—and essence laid out the entire case for—the 1982 kickoff of the great market in history. At the time it was published, the S&P 500 in March of 2000 it reached 1527. That’s a price gain of or 13.7% per year for almost 21 years (and those figures ignor whichtook the total gain to more than 28 times and the annualized total to 17.6%). The lesson is simple: investors should be leery Rather, it’s unpopularity that is the buyer’s friend. The Role of Companies

Companies, too, are subject to ups and downs in terms of succe on a series of cause-and-effect events. One of the most pronounced processes I’ve witnessed took place at Xerox. The office-copying giant—the first to make it possible to avoid the process of photostatting, whichrequired that documents be sent out lab to be duplicated—was one of the first companies I visited office-equipment analyst in the late 1960s. At that time, Xerox monopoly on “dry” copying and appeared to be in full command destiny. My senioranalyst and I used to meet with Xerox’s “anal and for each copier model in the product line he would the company’s projections in terms of the number of machines it in the field in the coming year and the annual rental revenue Because of Xerox’s dominant market position, it was largely those projections come true. It could charge monopolistic pricesthat enab it to fine-tune the size of its rental fleet and that gave it very margins. It also insisted on a rental-only model, refusing to machines and lose control of them. What a perpetual motion mach But Xerox management may have ignored the possibility that tho margins would prove unsustainable. In 1975, Xerox resolved an complaint over control of the copier market by entering into requiring it to make its powerful portfolio of patents available Competitors beganto produce and sell their own copiers. They were undercut Xerox’s pricesand displace part of its rental population. This reduced Xerox’s U.S. market share in copiers from nearly 100% teens, and it ate significantly into Xerox’s profits. As the incum had trouble responding to the price competition, as this would existing business. The competitors did so in an early example of calleddisruption. In 1968, thanks to its monopolistic position, strong growth profitability, Xerox was a leaderof the Nifty Fifty I described companies that were considered so strong and so fast growing that bad could happen” and “no stock price was too high.” But trees rarely the sky, and success is rarelyunending. Because Xerox’s behavior had invited competitionthat it was un to respond to—and for other reasons as well—by the early years century it was experiencing serious difficulty. Companies, like people, have the potential to respond to success behavior that dooms that very success. Thus companies can: get complacent and become “fat and happy,” become bureaucratic and slow-moving, fail to take actionto defend their positions,

cease to be innovative and non-conforming, and join the crowd mediocrity, and/or conclude that they can do prettymuch anything, and thus venture areas beyond their competence. In these ways and more, success truly carries the seeds of failure. good news—as I said earlier—is that failure also carries the seeds Once under attack, companies can regain their motivation and sense purpose. They can shed bureaucratic fat and get serious about meeting competitionand making money. And in the ultimate form of unsuccess, they can go through slim down, and shed losingbusiness lines, unprofitablelocations, onerous contracts and burdensomedebt. (Of course, however, the owners of companies that go into bankruptcy usually lose their positions.) Thus Jahan Janjigian wrote about Xerox in 2002: [In 2000,] a new management team implemented a number restructuring initiatives aimedat returning the company to profitability. Theseincluded aggressive cost cuts and the elimination of 13,600 positions. Xerox also sold off its Chinese and H operations, as well as 50% of Fuji Xerox to Fuji PhotoFilm. Furthermore, Xerox allowed GE Capital to take over the f receivables, for whichit already has received $2.7 billion. And X has exitedthe struggling small-officeand home-officebusiness. In April,Xerox agreed to pay $10 million to settle the year investigation. It later restated all of its past financial r required under the agreement. Additionally, the company was successful in working with creditors in renegotiating its debt obligations to more manageable terms. Perhaps most important, the company’s products are now more competitive in terms of price and quality. Due to the success of these efforts, Xerox returned to sooner than expected. . . . Givensignificant operational improvements, we think Xerox makes for a compelling buy a levels. (“Xerox Back from the Brink,” Forbes Growth Investo October 2002)

The Role of Timing Among the factors that can contribute greatly to an individual’ company’s success is timing. Among other things, it helps to thingsat an above-average time. That’s what put Xerox on the 1960s, and it helped me out as well. In August 1978, shortly after shifting from running Citibank’s eq research department to managing bond portfolios, I got the phonecall changed my life. “There’s a guy named Milken in California,” “and he’s involved in something calledhigh yield bonds. A client want portfolio of those bonds. Can you figureout what that means?” It was just in 1977 or 1978 that high yield bond investing had its institutionalinception. That’s when Michael Milken achieved his first in convincing investors that it’s okay for below-investment grade companies to issue bonds—andfor institutions to buy them—if the interest rate enough to compensate for the risk. The high yield universe consisted than $3 billion of bondsat the time I first got involved. The investing organizations had a rule against buying bondsrated below investment grade, whichwere commonly called“junk bonds.” And Moody’s categorically rejected B-rated bonds, saying they “fail to posse characteristics of a desirable investment.” How could these unpopular bondsnot have been underrated bargains? How could early participation not have been a boon? And then, a decade later, BruceKarshbrought his legal skills and str insight to my team, complementing Sheldon Stone’s expertise in we organized one of the first distressed debt funds from a majorfinan institution. What could be more risky and thus more unseemly than investing in the debt of companies in bankruptcy or considered destined for idea could people be more averse? In other words, whereelse could money be made so safely? In both these instances and more, I’ve been lucky to come across ass classes when they were still undiscovered, uncrowded and thus bargainpriced. There’s little that can make investing as easy as having largely to oneself. It beats the heck out of tryingto wringdecen a market that everybody has discovered, figured out, taken to and

into. The latter is far from a formula for success. The latecomer crowded field isn’t “the wise man in the beginning”; rather, he’s more likely to be “the fool in the end.” Thoseof us who’ve been lucky early ratherthan late know—or certainly should know—that our success wasn’t all our doing. The times have to cooperate. And that brings me to a great observation from Henry Phipps, celebrated partner of Andrew Carnegie and Henry Clay Frick,two century’s greatest businessmen. In 1899, Phipps wrote as follows: Good times like the present, make bad times;a law sure as the s of the pendulum. We have experience to know these elementary truths. Have we the sense to put them into practice? (George H Henry Clay Frick: The Man, 2002) As with people—whose successes may constitute isolated instances rat than indicators of great thingsto come as they mightbe wont to belie times may not remain conducive to continued success. Good times can encourage investment decisions that depend on the perpetuation of in order to succeed. But those good times can lead instead to bad test the decisions in ways they can’t withstand. Not only are good times followed sooner or later by bad times,bu with so many other examples of cycles—good times often produce bad Good times can lead to unwise debt issuance (as we saw in the the distressed debt cycle)or to overbuilding (as we saw in the the real estate cycle). Likewise as Ruchir Sharma, chief global strategist at Morgan Investment Management, wrote in his book The Rise and Fall of Natio regarding the effect of fresh new reformers, “Reform leads to growth good times,and good times encourage an arrogance and complacency that leads to a new crisis.” Phipps pointed out that history shows these trendsclearly. Th question is whether we are astuteand unemotional enough to times don’t necessarily lead to further good times,and thus that success can be cyclical. Let’s returnto Charlie Munger’s quote from Demosthenes: “For that wh a man wishes, that he will believe.” In other words, wishful takes hold. This can cause investors to believe that good times will be followed by more good times.But that ignores the cyclical nature and especially of success.

XVII THE FUTURE OF CYCLES The tendency of people to go to excess will never end. A since those excesses eventually have to correct, neither will the occurrence of cycles. Economies and markets have never moved in straight line in the past, and neither will they do so in th that means investors with the ability to understand cycles w opportunities for profit. Thus far I’ve covered a lot of the past and a bit of t conclude, I want to turn to the future. Over the course of my career, I’ve witnessed numerous occasion pundits said the occurrence of one type of cycle or another had end. Whether because of economic vitality, financial innovation, astut corporate management, or the supposed omniscience of central heads of Treasury, they observed that the fluctuations of either the econ cycle or the cycle in profits would be seen no more. I spent some time on this subject in “Will It Be Different Th (November 1996). First I described a newspaper article that had few days earlier: It recounted the case currently being made for this remaining a continuous, recession-free economic expansion. As its lead paragraph says: From boardroomsto livingrooms and from government offices to trading floors, a new consensus is emerging big, bad business cycle has been tamed. The current expansion, at 67 months, has already far exceed post-war average. Nevertheless, 51 of the 53 “top economists” surveyed by Blue Chip newsletter (my favorite experts and the s of my July 1996 memo, “The Valueof Predictions II”) predict g

next year of 1.5% or more. And the University of Michigan survey finds that among consumers, more expect five more good years than expect bad times to emerge. The Chairman of Sears states “There is no natural law that have to have a recession.” According to Amoco’s Chairman, “I d see any reason to believe [the recovery] can’t go on until th the century.” Sara Lee’s CEO says “I don’t know what could happen to make a cyclical downturn.” (“TheBusiness Cycleis Tamed, M Say, Alarming Others,” Wall StreetJournal, November 15, 1996) Clearly these statements, made in 1996, did not truly mark the end of cycles. Rather, there was a modest recession in 2001 and then, years later, the Great Recession of 2008–09, the most powerful cyclical ever experiencedby most people alive today. I went on in “Will It Be Different This Time?” to quote a assertions from other respected leaders: “There will be no interruption of our present prosperity.” “I cannot help but raise a dissenting voice to the statements prosperity in this country must necessarily diminish and recede in future.” “We are only at the beginning of a period that will go history as the golden age.” “The fundamental business of the country . . . is prosperous basis.” In assessing the reasonableness of these statements, it’s important to that they were made,respectively, by the president of the Pierce-Arro Car Company, the president of the New York Stock Exchange, the presi of the Bush Terminal Company, and the president of the United positions of the first and third should serve as a giveaway that statements came in the distant past, even without your knowing that president in question was Herbert Hoover. The timing of these 1928 and 1929—on the doorstep of the Great Depression that plagued worldfor more than a decade—was less than auspicious. So much, for prosperity without slowdowns, and for the end of cyclicality. But then, in the 2000s, the expectation of “permanent prosperity” rea head again.Whilenot specifically arguing that there would be no more cycles, a lot of investors, bankers and members of the medi embraced the belief that risk was gone—essentially the same thing. In his autobiography, StressTest, former Treasury secretary Tim Ge describes the climate when he arrived at the Fed in 2003:

Economists were starting to debate whether America’s long stretch stability constituted a new normal, a Great Moderation,a quasipermanent era of resilience to shocks. Therewas growing confide that derivatives and other financial innovations designed to hedgeand distribute risk—along with better monetary policy to respond to downturns and better technology to smooth out inventory cycles—had made devastating crises a thing of the past. The fact that this purportedly permanent prosperity was given a name lik “the Great Moderation” suggests it had entered the popular consc And thus it met my requirementfor the riskiest possible environmen whichthere’s widespread belief that there’s no risk. On page 120, I the mediaaccounts to that effect that enumerated the forcesthat had cause risk’s elimination:Fed omniscience, unremitting demand for securities from cash-flow-rich nations, and the latest inventions from Wall Street. Not only were all these “cycles are over” proclamations wrong, bu very much worthnoting that each of them coincided with—and certainly contributed to—the upward march to a cyclical high. And that followed those highs would be unusually painful: the Great Depression 1929–39, a three-year stock market decline in 2000–02 (the first since 1929)and the Global Financial Crisisof 2007–08. In “Will It Be Different This Time?” I went on from recalling Pollyanna statements just cited to drawing what I think are the essen conclusions on this subject: Of course, what these observations signaled wasn’t that cycles wouldn’t repeat, but ratherthat the onlookers had grown too confident. Cycles in economies, companies and markets will continu to occur at least as long as people are involved in making decisions—which I believe means forever. . . . Thereis a right time to argue that thingswill be that’s when the market is on its backside and everyone else is thingsat giveaway prices. But it’s been done before, and it’ll be done again. “It’s different this time” are four of the most dangerous wordsin the business world—especiallywhen applied, as is often the case, to some that has reached what in prior times would have been calledan extre Whenpeople say “it’s different” in this case, what they often mean is the rules and processes that produced cycles in the past have been

But the cyclical behavior of the financial past did not result from operation of physical or scientific rules. In science, cause and effec dependable and repeatable relationship, so that it’s possible to say confidence, “if a, then b.” But while there are some principles that opera the worldof finance and business, the resulting truth is very diffe that in science. The reason for this—as I’ve harped on repeatedly—is the inv people. People’s decisions have great influence on economic, business market cycles. In fact, economies, business and markets consist transactionsbetween people. And people don’t make their decisions scientifically. Some take history, facts and data into account, and some approach the decisions as “economic men.”But even the most unemotional and stoic among them are subject to human influences and the loss of Richard Feynman, the noted physicist, wrote, “Imagine how much ha physics would be if electrons had feelings!” That is, if elect they couldn’t be counted on to always do what science expe rules of physics would work only some of the time. The point is that people do have feelings, and as such they are inviolable laws. They’ll always bring emotions and foibles to their and investing decisions. As a result, they’ll become euphoric time and despondent at the wrong time—exaggerating the upside when thingsare going well and the downside risk when thingsare going poorly—and thus they’ll take trendsto cyclical extremes. A few paragraphs from The Most Important Thingprovide a good foundation for a recap of the main pointsregarding cycles’ persistence The basic reason for the cyclicality in our worldis the involve humans. Mechanical thingscan go in a straight line. Time moves aheadcontinuously. So can a machine when it’s adequately powere But processes in fields like history and economics involve people, when people are involved, the results are variable and cyclica main reason for this, I think, is that people are emotional a inconsistent, not steady and clinical. Objective factors do play a part in cycles, of course—fa quantitative relationships, worldevents, environmental changes, technological developments and corporate decisions. But it’s the application of psychology to these thingsthat causes investors to overreact or underreact, and thus determines the amplitude of the cyclical fluctuations. Whenpeople feel good about the way thingsare going and optimistic about the future, their behavior is strongly impacted. T

spendmore and save less. They borrow to increase their enjoyment o their profit potential, even though doing so makes their financial position more precarious (of course, concepts like precariousness a forgotten in optimistic times). And they become willing to p for current value or a piece of the future. Investors tend to look at the processes that are afoot, attribute me dependability to them, rely on that dependability, and extrapolate the processes. What they overlook is the role of emotions: greed on the and fear on the downswing. Emotions operate on cycles two ways:they magnify the forcestha extremes that eventually require correction, and they cause market participants to overlook the cyclicality of cyclical thingsat just those moments when recognition of excesses is most essential and most poten profitable: stage three of the bull and bear markets described on page 192 and 193–194. The following passage from The Most Important Thingmay serve to wr up regarding the outlook for cycles’ recurrence: Cycles will never stop occurring. If there were such a thing as a completely efficient market, and if people really made decisions in calculating and unemotional manner, perhaps cycles (or at le extremes) would be banished. But that’llnever be the case. Economies will wax and wane as consumers spendmore or less, responding emotionallyto economic factors or exogenous events, geopolitical or naturally occurring. Companies will anticipate a rosy futureduring the up cycle and thus over-expand facilities and inventories; these will become burdensomewhen the economy turns down. Providers of capital will be too generous when the e doing well, abetting over-expansion with cheapmoney, and then they’ll pull the reins too tight when thingscease to look as good. Investors will overvalue companies when they’re doing well and undervalue them when thingsget difficult. . . . People often act as if c that are doing well will do well forever, and investmentsthat are outperforming will outperform forever, and vice versa.Instead, it’s the opposite that is more likely to be true. A deep understanding of emotions and the overdoing they lead to key bottom lines of this book. Cyclical deviations from the trendline produced largely by overdoing and its eventual correction. This is

true in the securities markets, whichare nothing but a collection making decisions (oftenin a herd-like manner) that they hope will profitable. The first time rookie investors see this phenomenon occur, it understandable that they mightaccept that something that has never happened before—thecessation of cycles—could happen. But the second time or the third time, those investors, now experienced, should realize it’s never going to happen, and turn that realizat their advantage. (page 147) ∾ The key to dealing with the credit cycle lies in recognizing that it apex when thingshave been going well for a while, news has been good aversion is low, and investors are eager.That makes it easy for raise money and causes buyers and investors to compete for to provide it. It’s thingslike these that provide the margin of required for superior investing. Whenthese boxescan be ticked, inves should swinginto an aggressive mode. Superior investing doesn’t come from buying high-qualityassets, bu buying when the deal is good, the price is low, the potential retur substantial, and the risk is limited. Theseconditions are much more the when the credit markets are in the less-euphoric, more-stringent part cycle.The slammed-shut phase of the credit cycle probably does more to make bargains available than any other singlefactor. (pages 159–160) ∾ The merits of the asset in question matter only so much, can’t be strong enough to always carry the day. Human emot causes the pricesof assets—even worthwhile assets—to be transported levelsthat are extreme and unsustainable: either vertiginous highs or overl pessimistic lows. In short, conscientious belief in the inevitabilityof cycles like I’m means that a number of wordsand phrases must be excluded intelligent investor’s vocabulary. Theseinclude “never,” “always,” “forever,” “can’t,” “won’t,” “will”and “has to.” (pages 179–180) ∾ About 45 years ago—in the early 1970s—I received one of the was ever given, when an older and wiser investor introduced me to stagesof a bull market”: the first stage, when only a few unusually perceptive people believ thingswill get better, the second stage, when most investors realize that improvement actually taking place,and the third stage, when everyone concludes thingswill get better forever. The arrival of this simple truth opened my eyes to the no psychological extremes and the impact of those extremes on mark Like many of the great quotations and adages, it captures disproporti wisdom in a few simple words. It’s all about the changeabil

pattern they follow over the course of a cycle,and how they error. In the first stage, because the possibility of improvement is inv most investors and thus unappreciated, security pricesincorporate little or optimism. Often the first stage occurs after priceshave been pounded in crash,and the same downtrend that decimated pricesalso has wipedout psychology,turning the members of the crowd against the mark them to swearoff investing forever. In the last stage, on the other hand, events have gone well for so have been reflected so powerfully in asset prices, further liftingthe the market—that investors extrapolate improvement to infinity and pricesto reflect their optimism. Trees generally don’t grow to the sky, this stage investors act as if they will . . . and pay up they perceive. Few thingsare as costlyas paying for potential that have been overrated. It follows from the abovethat someone who invests in the fir when almost no one can see a reason for optimism—buys asset pricesfrom whichsubstantial appreciation is possible. But someone who in the third stage invariably pays a high price for the market’s exces enthusiasm and loses money as a result. Whena market is cascading downward, investors can often be he “We’re not going to try to catch a falling knife.” In other downward and there’s no way to know when it’ll stop, so why shou before we’re sure the bottom has been reached?” What I think they’ saying is, “We’re scared—in particular of buying before the stopped, and thus of looking bad—so we’re going to wait until the been reached, the dust has settled, and the uncertainty has been resol hopefully by now I’ve made it abundantly clear that when the dust has and investors’ nerves have steadied, the bargains will be gone. Once the slide has culm a bottom, by definition there are few sellers left to sell, and rally it’s buyers who predominate. Thus the selling dries up and buyers face growing competition. (pages 235–237) ∾ Exiting the market after a decline—and thus failing to partic rebound—is truly the cardinal sin in investing. Experiencing a market loss in the downward phase of a cycle isn’t fatal in and long as you hold through the beneficial upward part as well. It’s that downward fluctuation into a permanent loss by selling out at that’s really terrible. Thus understanding cycles and having the emotional and financial wherewithal needed to live through them is an essential ingre investment success. And yet security pricesgenerally fluctuate much more than earnings. Th reasons, of course, are largely psychological, emotional and non-f Thus price changes exaggerate and overstate fundamental changes. (page 186)

The truth is that financial facts and figures are only a starting market behavior; investor rationality is the exception, not the rule; market spends little of its time calmly weighing financial data pricesfree of emotionality. Whenpeople say “it’s different” in this case, what they mean is tha rules and processes that produced cycles in the past have been suspe the cyclical behavior of the financial past did not result from the physical or scientific rules. In science, cause and effect enjoy a depe and repeatable relationship, so that it’s possible to say with confi then b.” But while there are some principles that operate in the world finance and business, the resulting truth is very different from that The reason for this—as I’ve harped on repeatedly—is the inv people. And people don’t make their decisions scientifically. People have feelings, and as such they aren’tbound by inviolabl They’ll always bring emotions and foibles to their economic and decisions. As a result, they’ll become euphoric at the wron despondent at the wrong time—exaggerating the upside potential are going well and the downside risk when thingsare going poorly—and they’ll take trendsto cyclical extremes. As It’s hard to make frequent distinctions and hard to do so correc Thus distinctions in the middle ground aren’tas potentially profita at the extremes, and those distinctions can’t be expected to work dependably.

Detecting and exploiting the extremes is really the best we can ho And I believe it can be done dependably—if you’re analytical, experienced(or well-versed in history) and unemotional. That means, however, that you shouldn’t expect to reach profitable conclusions daily monthly or even yearly. The reasonableness of the effort at cycle timing depends simply you expect of it. About the Author HOWARD M ARKS is cochairman and cofounder of Oaktree Capital Management, a leading investment firm responsible for over $120 billio assets. His previous book on investing, The Most Important Thing: Uncommon Sense for the Thoughtful Investor, was a critically acclaimed bestseller. He lives in New York City.
